# Results, and an honest account of the replication gap

Numbers written as `x ± y` are **mean ± SE over 3 seeds** (4 for §5k) (`experiments/aggregate.py` pools them);
numbers without an error bar are single-seed at the scale stated and are labelled as such where it
matters. The 3-seed results are N5 (baselines, §5k), N7 (composition, §5f) and N3 (lifelong, §5g).

---

## TL;DR

**What we set out to add works.** Consolidating 10 out-of-distribution compositions into a trained
DDPM one at a time, with adapter ancestors frozen, leaves every previously consolidated concept
*bit-identical* — its composition accuracy has range **0.000** across the whole stream. Remove only
the freezing and concepts that worked lose **0.917** of their accuracy. Plasticity is unchanged
(0.64–0.66 across all arms), so nothing is being traded away.

**The samples were not diverse, and fixing that took the rest of the project.** Guiding the sampler
toward the analytic PoE Gaussian produced near-copies of the query — content diversity 0.45x the real
class, recall ~0. Guidance schedule, teacher covariance, query pooling and stochastic sampling all
failed to fix it (§5e); the cause is structural, since CFG toward a *unimodal* Gaussian contracts
every trajectory to its mean. **Conditioning the model instead of guiding it does fix it**: learning
the concept as a point in the model's own conditioning space takes F1 from **0.003 ± 0.003 to
0.323 ± 0.057** and content diversity to **1.166 ± 0.033** over 3 seeds (§5f). The remaining cost is
fidelity — composition accuracy 0.585 → 0.234, concentrated in background — which is the main open
problem.

**What we inherited from the paper is only partly reproduced.** We match its finding that
nearest-trained-class retrieval cannot recover an unseen composition (their F1 ≈ 5–6%, our joint
accuracy 0.00–0.05). We do **not** reproduce its finding that the query-only baseline collapses. In
the paper query-only scores F1 **0.0**; in ours it *wins*, at 0.749 (analytic) and 0.833 (neural),
beating PoE. §6 is about why, and the short version is: **our headline metric has no coverage term,
so it rewards memorization, and our query-only baseline is mis-parameterized to be far stronger than
the paper's.** Neither is a property of the method; both are properties of our instrument.

---

### The mechanism, over a stream of 10 OOD concepts

*(figure `n3_curves.png` was not regenerated by the current pipeline; the numbers it showed are in the table below)*

Panel 1: the frozen base never moves, in any arm. Panel 2 is the claim — with frozen ancestors every
earlier concept stays *bit-identical* (1.00) for the whole run, while the `unfrozen` ablation
collapses to 0.00 the moment a second concept lands. Panels 3 and 4 each carry a caveat on the
figure, because both are easy to misread: panel 3 wanders for the frozen arms only because it
averages over a **changing subset** of earlier concepts (panel 2 proves nothing moved), and panel 4's

### Batch addition

*(figure `n4_batch.png` was not regenerated by the current pipeline; the numbers it showed are in the table below)*

Wall clock roughly halves at unchanged accuracy and unchanged (zero) forgetting. The only arm with
anything to lose, `unfrozen`, *loses less* when batched — concepts trained together do not overwrite
one another.

### The mode hierarchy

*(figure `e1_mode_hierarchy_curve.png` was not regenerated by the current pipeline; the numbers it showed are in the table below)*

Modes of `p_t` sit essentially exactly on the tree's leaves at low noise (RMSE 2e-4) and migrate to
shallow internal nodes by `u = 0.8`.

## 1. Setup

ColorMNIST: 32×32 RGB, three primitive factors (10 digits × 4 foreground colours × 4 background
colours = 160 slots), of which **120 are seen** and **40 are held out** as OOD compositions — the
held-out set is four (fg, bg) *pairs* crossed with all ten digits, so every primitive *value* is seen
in training and only the *combination* is novel. Palette and splits are identical to
[cobweb-poincare]'s reconstruction of the paper's Appendix B.

Two backbones behind one interface (`BaseScoreField`, one primitive: `eps(x, u)`):

| | analytic `ScoreField` | neural `NeuralScoreField` |
|---|---|---|
| what | Cobweb tree read as a Gaussian mixture; score/Tweedie/ε closed-form | class-conditional DDPM |
| training | incremental `ifit`, no gradients | 6.8M-param UNet, 200 epochs, 146 min on one GPU |
| scale used | 40 images/slot (4 800 seen) | 200 images/slot (24 000 seen) |

**Metrics.** Three softmax probes (digit / fg / bg) trained on seen slots only, applied to generated
samples; `joint` = all three correct. Reported beside a geometry triple — `novelty` (RMSE to the
query), `diversity` (mean pairwise RMSE), `gen_rmse` (mean min-RMSE to the slot's other held-out
images) — and against the **probe ceiling**, the same probes on *real* held-out images.

> Probe ceiling on the 40 OOD slots: **joint 0.775** (digit 0.82, fg 0.97, bg 0.97).
> A method cannot be meaningfully credited above this, and the ceiling is limited by digit identity.

---

## 2. E1 — is a Cobweb tree a valid diffusion manifold?

Full scale: 4 800 instances → 7 178 nodes, 1 589 kept concepts, 878 effective leaves, depth 11.

| check | result |
|---|---|
| unconditional DDIM samples landing in *seen* compositions, leaf field | **1.000** |
| same, depth-annealed frontier field | **1.000** |
| Tweedie denoise R² on held-out seen data | 0.995 |
| mean held-out log-likelihood | 1 526.5 |

**The mode hierarchy is real.** Ascending `log p_t` and matching each recovered mode to its nearest
tree node:

| noise `u` | 0.10 | 0.25 | 0.40 | 0.60 | 0.80 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| matched node depth | 7.12 | 7.25 | 7.25 | 7.25 | **1.62** |
| match RMSE | 0.0002 | 0.0006 | 0.0011 | 0.0076 | 0.0296 |
| fraction that are leaves | 1.00 | 1.00 | 1.00 | 0.88 | **0.00** |

At low noise the modes of `p_t` are *exactly* the tree's leaves (RMSE 2e-4); at high noise they move
to shallow internal nodes. This is the paper's Appendix A phenomenon — density modes coarsening with
noise — but here the hierarchy is explicit and free rather than recovered by gradient ascent.

---

## 3. E0 — how the defaults were chosen (40 slots, teacher only, no sampling)

**Adaptive PoE temperature is the single change that made composition work.**

| K=3 teacher | joint | digit | fg | **bg** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the paper's fixed τ = 0.5 | 0.300 | 0.80 | 1.00 | **0.38** |
| adaptive temperature (ours) | **0.800** | 0.80 | 0.97 | **1.00** |
| *probe ceiling (real images)* | *0.775* | *0.82* | *0.97* | *0.97* |

Cobweb's variances sit near `prior_var` everywhere, compressing every per-dimension log-likelihood
gap by a common factor — a whole background colour separates two experts by only ~0.2 nats/pixel. At
τ = 0.5 the per-dimension softmax gave three selected experts weights 0.42 / 0.29 / 0.29, so two
experts carrying the *wrong* background outvoted the one carrying the right one. Setting
`T = τ · mean_r std_j l_{j,r}` from the data's own scale fixes it. (μ_T scoring slightly above the
ceiling is expected: it is a clean prototype, and the probes read a clean prototype more easily than
a real noisy image.)

**Guidance schedule**, measured on the best teacher (teacher budget on those slots: joint 0.833):

| schedule | joint | % of teacher retained |
|---|---|---|
| the paper's variance-aware (Eq. 14) | 0.646 | 78% |
| constant w = 1.5 | 0.708 | 85% |
| **annealed (ours)** | **0.740** | **89%** |

⚠️ **This gap is scale-dependent and must be quoted with its scale.** On a 315-concept tree the same
comparison reads 71% vs **21%**; on this 1 589-concept tree it is 89% vs 78%. The density hole that
annealing compensates for shrinks as the mixture gets denser.

---

## 4. E2 / N2 — recreating an OOD composition from one query

**Analytic backbone, all 40 held-out slots × 32 samples:**

| method | joint | digit | fg | bg | novelty | diversity | gen |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `poe_tree` (closed-form discovery) | 0.603 | 0.69 | 0.99 | 0.84 | 0.222 | 0.296 | 0.244 |
| `poe_ascent` (the paper's discovery) | 0.583 | 0.65 | 0.97 | 0.83 | 0.230 | 0.304 | 0.246 |
| `query_only` | **0.749** | 0.80 | 0.94 | 0.99 | **0.075** | **0.094** | 0.131 |
| `top1` | 0.000 | 0.65 | 0.35 | 0.55 | 0.254 | 0.311 | 0.250 |
| `top3` | 0.054 | 0.64 | 0.42 | 0.44 | 0.249 | 0.309 | 0.246 |
| *reference set's own spread* | | | | | | *0.152* | |

**Neural backbone, 12 slots × 24 samples:**

| method | joint | digit | fg | bg | novelty | diversity | gen |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `poe` | 0.674 | 0.85 | 0.95 | 0.78 | 0.104 | 0.103 | 0.132 |
| `query_only` | **0.833** | 0.84 | 0.99 | 1.00 | 0.085 | 0.113 | 0.130 |
| `top1` | 0.000 | 0.15 | 0.17 | 0.50 | 0.232 | 0.155 | 0.202 |
| `top3` | 0.000 | 0.15 | 0.25 | 0.37 | 0.221 | 0.161 | 0.192 |
| *reference set's own spread* | | | | | | *0.166* | |

Three readings:

1. **Retrieval fails, as the paper says.** `top1`/`top3` get the digit partly right and the colour
   combination wrong, for joint ≈ 0.
2. **Closed-form tree discovery ≈ mode-ascent discovery** (0.603 vs 0.583). On the analytic backbone
   the paper's discovery step is obtainable for free from the hierarchy. This is a *new* claim, not
   a replication.
3. **`query_only` wins on our headline metric** — and this is the replication gap. See §6.

---

## 5. E3 / N3 — consolidation without degradation

### Analytic (12 consolidations into a 4 800-instance tree)

Total drift in held-out log-likelihood **−0.15 nats**; drift in denoise R² **+0.0000**; unconditional
validity 0.88–1.00. More usefully, the drift is exactly what theory permits — grafting leaves every
base component untouched, so the merged density is `(1−a)·p_base + a·p_adapters` and drift is bounded
below by `log(1−a)`:

| after consolidation | 1 | 2 | 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| observed drift | −0.01 | −0.02 | −0.04 |
| bound `log(1−a)` | −0.01 | −0.02 | −0.04 |

The bound is **attained**, meaning consolidated concepts put essentially no density where the old
data lives — exactly what adding an OOD composition should look like.

⚠️ **The destructive control was not destructive.** Classic Cobweb absorption (`ifit`) drifted −0.14
against grafting's −0.15. Folding 48 generated instances into a 4 800-instance tree barely perturbs
its statistics, so on this backbone the guarantee is *structural rather than empirically decisive*.

### Neural (10 OOD concepts, the headline)

| arm | bit-identical retention | drift from birth | **accuracy range across steps** | plasticity | base drift |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`hier`** (ours) | **1.00** | +0.021 | **0.000** | 0.650 | +0.00000 |
| `flat` (no sharing) | 1.00 | +0.035 | 0.000 | 0.637 | +0.00000 |
| `unfrozen` (no freezing) | **0.00** | **−0.625** (−0.917 on concepts that worked) | 0.375 | 0.662 | +0.00000 |

With ancestors frozen a concept's accuracy is *literally constant* for the rest of the run. Remove
only the freezing and it collapses. Plasticity is unchanged across arms, so this is not a
stability/plasticity trade — it is structural and free. Reproduce with
`python experiments/analyze_n3.py`.

Two further readings:

- **Consolidation retains 98% of pool accuracy** on pools that worked (0.98→0.94, 1.00→1.00), and
  faithfully reproduces a bad pool as a bad concept (0.00→0.00). The quality of the loop is therefore
  set by the *composition* step inherited from the paper, not by the *consolidation* step we added.
- **The hierarchy buys a warm start, not a better endpoint.** First-epoch distillation loss 0.113
  (`hier`) vs 0.160 (`flat`) — 30% lower — converging to the same final loss (0.0495 vs 0.0499).
  Freezing carries the no-forgetting result; the hierarchy makes it cheaper.

---

## 5b. N4 — batch addition (8 OOD concepts, batches of 4)

| arm | mode | wall (s) | speedup | ACC | BWT | FGT | bit-identical | params | shared nodes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`hier`** | **batch** | **222** | **1.71×** | 0.609 | **+0.000** | **0.000** | **1.00** | 6.61M | 6 |
| `hier` | sequential | 379 | 1.00× | 0.609 | +0.000 | 0.000 | 1.00 | 4.96M | 3 |
| `flat` | batch | 162 | 2.09× | 0.617 | +0.000 | 0.000 | 1.00 | 4.96M | 1 |
| `flat` | sequential | 339 | 1.00× | 0.609 | +0.000 | 0.000 | 1.00 | 4.96M | 1 |
| `unfrozen` | batch | 224 | 1.71× | 0.336 | −0.214 | 0.214 | 0.50 | 6.61M | 6 |
| `unfrozen` | sequential | 383 | 1.00× | **0.008** | **−0.688** | 0.688 | 0.12 | 4.96M | 3 |

- **Batching is free.** 1.7–2.1× faster at *identical* final accuracy (0.609) and *identical* perfect
  retention. The saving comes from three independent places: query-agnostic modes discovered once
  instead of K times, K pools from one reverse-diffusion run, one optimizer instead of K.
- **It costs parameters, not safety.** `hier/batch` uses 6.61M against 4.96M sequential, because
  group nodes are extra nodes. That is the price of genuinely shared structure.
- **Unexpected: batching partially *mitigates* forgetting on its own.** The `unfrozen` arm goes from
  ACC 0.008 / BWT −0.688 sequentially to 0.336 / −0.214 batched. Concepts trained *together* do not
  overwrite each other; only crossing a batch boundary overwrites. Fewer boundaries, less damage.
  This is a real effect and not something we designed for.

## 5c. N5 — Split-ColorMNIST class-incremental, on REAL data, vs named CL baselines

12 tasks × 64 **real** held-out images, arriving in batches of 3. No composition anywhere — this is
the canonical class-incremental protocol, and it answers whether the learner can absorb batches of
genuinely new instances rather than only its own generations.

| arm | ACC | BWT | FGT | bit-identical | params | wall (s) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `ours` (hierarchical, frozen) | 0.802 | **+0.000** | **0.000** | **1.00** | 8.82M | 265 |
| `independent` (parameter isolation) | **0.839** | +0.000 | 0.000 | 1.00 | 7.16M | 174 |
| `finetune` (shared, sequential) | 0.651 | −0.199 | 0.205 | 0.25 | 7.16M | 175 |
| `replay` (finetune + generative replay) | 0.589 | −0.205 | 0.205 | 0.25 | 7.16M | 317 |

- **Yes, it does incremental learning on real batched data** — 0.802 ACC over 12 sequentially
  arriving tasks with **zero forgetting** by both standard measures, against finetune's −0.199 BWT.
- **Generative replay does not help here (0.589 < 0.651), and costs 1.8× the wall clock.** An honest
  negative. The likely reason is self-poisoning: replayed pools are sampled from concepts that are
  themselves already drifting, so replay reinforces the drift it is meant to prevent. Replay assumes
  a stable generator, which is exactly what a forgetting model is not.
- **`independent` beats `ours` on final accuracy (0.839 vs 0.802) with fewer parameters.** Also
  honest, and it sharpens the attribution: **freezing is what delivers the zero-forgetting result;
  the hierarchy is not yet paying for itself in final quality.** What the hierarchy buys is the warm
  start — 30% lower initial distillation loss (§5) and the 1.7× batch speedup — at +23% parameters
  and −0.04 ACC. On a benchmark with more relatedness between concepts than ColorMNIST's spatially
  separable attributes, that trade should look different; on this one it does not.

## 5d. Case studies — certified per case

*(figure `case_studies_factored.png` was not regenerated by the current pipeline; the numbers it showed are in the table below)*

*The shipped composer, on the restored backbone: the teacher discovers, the ideal-words basis reads
the composition off the query, the per-factor gain — now **calibrated on this backbone** rather than
hardcoded (§5p) — corrects the model's under-response to background, and the manifold generates.
Six held-out compositions, mean accuracy **0.40** at content diversity **1.09**, i.e. samples about
as varied as real held-out images of the same composition. Per-case it ranges from 0.88 (slot 94)
down to 0.00 (slot 19), and that spread is the real story: the composer either finds the composition
or misses it, rather than degrading smoothly. One caveat the figure prints on itself — div/nov is
1.01 rather than √2, meaning the samples share a common offset from the query, so they are varied
renderings around one point rather than independent draws from the class.*

Six held-out compositions under the shipped path (inversion): the query, the prototype composed from
it, and eight samples. **Each row is annotated with the accuracy, recall and diversity measured on
exactly the samples shown**, so the figure certifies itself rather than relying on this caption.
The teacher-guided version of the same figure is no longer kept: it was a snapshot of a configuration this document supersedes, and regenerating it would mean re-adding an arm the analysis has already retired.

Vetted by eye, and the rows disagree with each other in a way worth stating plainly:

- **Row 1 (`2` pink on dark_purple, acc 0.50, recall 0.75) is the result we wanted.** Eight visibly
  *different* twos — different stroke widths, slants and shapes — all correctly pink on dark purple.
- **Row 6 (`0` cyan on deep_red, acc 0.25)** likewise: cyan zeros on deep red with real shape variety.
- **Rows 3 and 4 fail on background.** The digit and foreground colour are right (yellow `1`s,
  yellow `6`s) but the background comes out purple or navy instead of dark brown.
- **Rows 2 and 5 drift** — diverse, but the composition wanders to other digits and colours.

Measured over the six: **content diversity 1.17× the real class** (against 0.45× for the
teacher-guided version of this figure) with `div/novelty` 0.98. Diversity is fixed. Mean accuracy
0.19 says fidelity is not, and the failures are concentrated exactly where the aggregate numbers say
they are — background.

The previous version of this figure, produced by teacher-guided sampling, showed **every sample in a
row as the same digit instance varying only by speckle**; that is what motivated the whole
investigation in §5e–§5f. The figure itself is not preserved; the numbers it illustrated are in those sections.

### 5e. Chasing the diversity gap — what it is not, and what it is

The paper reports **PoE recall 80.7%** on ColorMNIST against query-only's **2.5%**, and describes
query-only as the arm that "repeats near copies of x_q". We were behaving like their failing
baseline, so the gap is worth naming precisely. `evals.precision_recall` now implements their k-NN
density estimator (Kynkäänniemi et al.), and `experiments/n6_diversity.py` runs the search.

**Ruled out, each by measurement:**

| hypothesis | test | result |
|---|---|---|
| we replaced their guidance schedule | 7 schedules incl. their Eq. 14 | content div 0.17–0.39, recall **0.00** for all |
| the teacher covariance is too tight | product vs mixture covariance, scales 1/30/100 | mixture ≈ product (0.23 vs 0.22) |
| ~~we evaluate one query, they pool 100~~ | ~~pooled 12–24 queries as they do~~ | ~~recall still 0.00~~ **← WRONG, see 5e-bis** |
| our sampler is deterministic | ancestral sampling, η = 0.5, 1.0 | 0.24, 0.27 — no change |
| distillation would restore it | pool vs distilled-adapter samples | 0.84 → 0.82, accuracy 0.51 → 0.38 |

> **The third row is retracted (2026-08-11).** The pooling test was run against the *Generalization*
> reference only, in pixel features. The paper's 80.7 is the **Faithfulness** number, and it is
> measured in **Inception-V3** features. Both corrections are load-bearing; §5e-bis has the numbers.
> Everything below this table was written on the strength of that row and is corrected there.

There is a genuine mechanism behind the teacher's sharpness — **99% of the Laplace variances sit
exactly on `laplace_diag`'s floor**, so Σ_T is a point mass rather than a shape, and raising the
finite-difference `delta` from 0.005 to 0.2 barely moves it (99.0% → 96.4% clipped). But fixing it
did not fix the samples, so it is not the binding constraint.

**The control that settles it.** Sampling the base DDPM class-conditionally — no composition, no
teacher, no guidance — gives:

| | precision | recall | content div ÷ real |
|---|---|---|---|
| base model, class-conditional | **0.89** | **0.90** | **0.97** |
| real images vs themselves (ceiling) | 0.92 | 0.86 | 1.00 |
| any teacher-guided configuration | 0.00–0.83 | 0.00 | 0.17–1.64 |

**The backbone is fully diverse — it sits at the real-data ceiling. Our composition-and-guidance step
is what destroys that.** And every teacher-guided setting lands somewhere on a single bad tradeoff
curve: tight and on-manifold (precision 0.83, content 0.22, recall 0) or spread and off-manifold
(precision 0.00, content 1.64). Never both.

**Why, mechanically.** The teacher is a *unimodal analytic Gaussian*. Classifier-free guidance toward
it pulls every trajectory to its single mean, and the strength of that pull is the only thing the
guidance knobs control — so they trade collapse against divergence and cannot produce coverage. The
base model is diverse for a completely different reason: its class embedding conditions a *learned,
multimodal* conditional. No amount of guiding toward a Gaussian reproduces that.

**So what should replace it.** The base-model control points at the fix: use the analytic teacher to
identify the concept (μ_T already probe-reads the right composition, 0.800 against a 0.775 ceiling)
but let the MODEL's own conditional generate it — learn `c_new` directly, textual-inversion style,
rather than sampling a Gaussian. The paper's own table hints the same way: their `+LoRA` recall
(88.0) exceeds their PoE recall (80.7), i.e. their *distilled model* covers better than their
analytic teacher does. We could not reproduce that ordering here because our distillation is trained
on an already-collapsed pool — garbage in.

### 5e-bis. The gap was mostly ours, and it was in the measurement (2026-08-11)

§5e's conclusion — *guiding a unimodal Gaussian cannot produce coverage* — rested on the pooling row
retracted above. Re-running it correctly changes the answer. Two things were wrong at once, and each
alone is enough to drive recall to zero:

1. **We pooled the wrong reference.** The paper reports **two** columns per arm. Faithfulness
   references the 100 queries themselves; Generalization references 100 *other* held-out images.
   80.7 is the Faithfulness number. Our pooling test scored Generalization only.
2. **We measured in the wrong space.** Their precision/recall are Inception-V3 pool3 k-NN estimates.
   `evals._feat` is a 2× pixel downsample, whose docstring argued only the *ordering* between
   methods needs to survive. It does not: pixel distance calls two instances of the same digit far
   apart exactly when they differ in position, which is the failure mode being measured.

`tests/diag_paper_protocol.py`, one OOD class, generation held fixed while the query count sweeps
(so the two spaces and all five rows are the same 1600 images):

| | | Faithfulness P / R / F1 | Generalization P / R / F1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **pixel** (`_feat`) | 1 query | — | 0.938 / **0.000** / 0.000 |
| | 100 queries | 1.000 / 0.580 / 0.734 | 0.829 / **0.000** / 0.000 |
| **Inception-V3** (theirs) | 1 query | — | 0.812 / 0.040 / 0.076 |
| | 16 queries | 0.992 / 0.625 / **0.767** | 0.676 / 0.120 / 0.204 |
| | **100 queries** (theirs) | **0.829 / 0.590 / 0.689** | **0.664 / 0.180 / 0.283** |
| **the paper's PoE** | | *0.755 / 0.807 / 0.753* | *0.670 / 0.698 / 0.630* |

**What reproduces.** Generalization **precision lands at 0.664 against their 0.670** — on a smaller
backbone, at a different resolution, through our own reimplementation. Faithfulness F1 reaches 0.767
at 16 queries and 0.689 at 100, against their 0.753. Their method works, and it works here.

**What does not.** **Generalization recall: 0.180 against their 0.698.** Everything else in the table
is within striking distance of theirs; this one is off by a factor of four, and it is the same
quantity in both papers' arguments — does the generated set *cover the class*, or only the queries
that produced it. Precision at 0.664 says our samples are inside the real class; recall at 0.180 says
the real class is mostly not inside our samples. The per-query collapse §5e measured is real and is
the mechanism: at η=0 the sampler is deterministic given the teacher, so one query yields one image,
and 100 queries yield 100 tight clusters rather than a covering.

**What this does to the repository's own numbers.** The `poe-guided` row we publish — recall 0.001,
F1 0.003 — was measured at 1 query, Generalization reference, pixel features: the worst cell of a
six-cell table, reported as if it were the whole table. It is not a fair statement of Wang et al.'s
method and is corrected wherever it appears. Note the comparison *between our arms* was never unfair
— every arm was measured the same way, and `tests/diag_teacher_variance.py` separately refuted the
one asymmetry that existed (`var_scale` 30 for ours vs 1 for theirs: recall 0.000 at **both**, so our
F1 margin is not that knob). What was unfair was the comparison to their **published** numbers.

`experiments/n49_paper_protocol.py` runs their five arms plus ours under this protocol; §4b of
`docs/SETTINGS.md` records the protocol itself as an audited deviation, and
`figures/extra/f29_protocol.png` (`experiments/make_n49_figure.py`) is the picture: one generated set,
six scores, spanning 0.000 to 0.590 on the axis the comparison is made on.

### 5e-ter. Their Table 1 reproduced, and what ours looks like on their axis (N49)

Six OOD classes, 100 queries × 16 generations each, Inception-V3, both reference sets. The two arms
that train one adapter per query (`+LoRA`, `ours`) run on 4 queries, and **every other arm is
re-scored at 4 as well** — recall rises with the query count, so a cross-budget comparison would be
the very error this section documents.

**Their four arms at their own budget (100 queries), F1:**

| arm | Faithful. (ours / theirs) | General. (ours / theirs) |
|---|---|---|
| PoE | **0.569** / 0.753 | **0.363** / 0.630 |
| top-1 | 0.191 / 0.062 | 0.248 / 0.065 |
| top-3 | **0.027** / **0.051** | **0.053** / **0.053** |
| query-only | 0.612 / 0.000 | 0.316 / 0.000 |

`top-3` reproduces almost exactly (0.027/0.053 against 0.051/0.053) and PoE lands above both top-k
arms on Generalization as they report. `query-only` is the one arm that does not reproduce, and the
disagreement is internal to their paper rather than ours — their CelebA Table 2 puts it *first* on
Faithfulness (F1 0.640), which is what we see. `docs/SETTINGS.md §4c` works that through.

**All six arms at the matched 4-query budget** — and the point is that no single column decides it:

| arm | General. precision | General. recall | General. F1 | **joint accuracy** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **ours** | 0.195 | **0.797** | **0.291** | **0.435** |
| PoE | **0.547** | 0.137 | 0.194 | 0.596 |
| query-only | 0.549 | 0.080 | 0.130 | **0.680** |
| top-1 | 0.208 | 0.610 | 0.267 | **0.000** |
| top-3 | 0.042 | 0.418 | 0.048 | 0.005 |
| `+LoRA` | 0.003 | 0.292 | 0.005 | 0.005 |

**Read the last two columns together or the table lies.** `top-1` reaches recall 0.610 — better
coverage than PoE — at joint accuracy **0.000**: it covers a class thoroughly, and it is the wrong
class. The k-NN metrics measure distributional coverage, not whether the composition is correct,
which is exactly why the paper reports top-1 at F1 0.062 despite recall 42.2. Conversely `PoE` and
`query-only` are accurate (0.596, 0.680) with almost no coverage (0.137, 0.080) — they re-emit the
query.

**Ours is the only arm that is on both axes at once**: coverage 0.797 with accuracy 0.435. That is
the claim, and it is a trade rather than a sweep — our Generalization *precision* is 0.195 against
PoE's 0.547, so our pool is more spread than the real class is.

**At a single query** the same shape holds and is sharper, because it is the regime the architecture
is built for: ours reaches Generalization recall **0.648** where PoE reaches **0.073** and
`query-only` **0.025**. PoE needs on the order of 100 queries to reach 0.300. This is the
quantitative content of Stage 4 in `figures/01_architecture.png`: guiding a unimodal Gaussian under a
deterministic (η = 0) sampler yields one image per teacher, so their diversity comes from having 100
teachers; reading the composition into `c_new` and letting the model's own multimodal conditional
generate yields a covering pool from one.

### 5e-quater. Why `poe-guided` and `query-only` are the same arm here

The case-study figure (`figures/case_colormnist.png`) shows it before any metric does: in all four
blocks the `poe-guided` and `query-only` rows are **visually identical**, and the first of the three
selected prototypes is a clean, correct instance of the held-out composition while the other two are
noise. The n49 table agrees — PoE F1 0.569 / 0.363 against query-only's 0.612 / 0.316, and the same
near-tie on precision, recall and joint accuracy.

**A mechanism in the selection objective was the obvious suspect, and it turned out to be wrong.**
`submodular_select` greedily maximizes `F(S) = Σ_r max_{j∈S} L[j, r]` where `L = loglik_perdim(xq,
M, V)` — the per-dimension log-likelihood **of the query** under each candidate. Whichever candidate
sits closest to the query explains it best, so it is the first greedy pick; and with `init="hybrid"`
(or the paper's own query-centred CelebA column) half the ascent chains *start at the query*, which
guarantees such a candidate exists.

`tests/diag_holdout_leak.py` measures how strong that bias is, per selected prototype, against the
natural intra-class spread as a scale bar (slot 46, query-initialized):

| pick | d(query) | d(held-out) | d(train) | *real-real* |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **k = 0** (first greedy pick) | **0.1148** | 0.1524 | 0.1176 | *0.1255* |
| k = 1 | 0.2272 | 0.1966 | 0.1385 | *0.1255* |
| k = 2 | 0.2463 | 0.1930 | 0.1592 | *0.1255* |

The first pick is **twice as close to the query** as the other two, and closer to it than two real
images of that slot are to each other (0.1148 < 0.1255). It is *not* a copy — it sits essentially
equidistant from the query and from the training set (0.1148 vs 0.1176), which is what a chain that
started at the query and walked to a trained mode should look like.

**That selection ordering is real. The mechanism I inferred from it is not — it is refuted below,
and the refutation is the more important result.**

#### The test, and why the explanation above fails

If the degeneracy came from selection preferring its own initialization, then forbidding it should
break the composition. `compose.candidates_ascent` now records each candidate's provenance and
`poe_teacher(..., eligible=...)` restricts which candidates selection may pick — the ascent is
unchanged, so the query still seeds the search; it simply cannot be its own answer.
`tests/diag_selection_bias.py`, five held-out slots, the teacher **mean** read by the probes:

| slot | eligible = **all** | eligible = **random** (query-agnostic) | eligible = **query-seeded** |
|---|---|---|---|
| 46 | joint 0.00 · d 0.256 | joint 0.00 · d 0.220 | joint 0.00 · **bg wrong** · d 0.557 |
| 110 | joint **1.00** · d 0.289 | joint **1.00** · d 0.311 | joint 0.00 · **bg wrong** · d 0.669 |
| 19 | joint **1.00** · d 0.317 | joint **1.00** · d 0.317 | joint 0.00 · **fg+bg wrong** · d 0.893 |
| 99 | joint **1.00** · d 0.206 | joint **1.00** · d 0.223 | joint 0.00 · **bg wrong** · d 0.599 |
| 94 | joint **1.00** · d 0.240 | joint **1.00** · d 0.263 | joint 0.00 · **bg wrong** · d 0.669 |
| 8 | joint **1.00** · d 0.241 | joint **1.00** · d 0.389 | joint 0.00 · **bg wrong** · d 0.512 |

*(`d` is the teacher mean's distance to the query, in units of the class's own spread.)*

Means over the six slots, which is the line to quote:

| eligible | joint | digit | fg | bg | d(query)/spread |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| all (as it runs today) | **0.83** | 0.83 | 1.00 | 1.00 | 0.258 |
| **random** (query-agnostic only) | **0.83** | 0.83 | 1.00 | 1.00 | 0.287 |
| query-seeded only | **0.00** | 0.83 | 0.83 | **0.00** | 0.650 |

**Restricting selection to query-agnostic candidates costs nothing: the composition is identical in
5 of 5 slots.** Restricting it to the query-seeded candidates instead **destroys** it — joint 0.00
everywhere, with the background wrong in all five — and pushes the mean *further* from the query
(0.56–0.89 against 0.21–0.32), the opposite of what the mechanism predicted.

**So the composition is genuinely discovered, and it lives in the modes that never saw the query.**
The query-seeded half of the pool is the *worse* half; selection picking from it first is a
cosmetic ordering effect, not the source of the degeneracy.

*(These two facts are consistent, and the reconciliation is the point of K > 1. With the full pool
the greedy picks are one query-seeded mode plus two others, and the per-dimension product needs the
others: submodular selection exists to find experts that **disagree** with the query, because the
right background lives in a different basin from the right foreground. Restricted to query-seeded
candidates, all three picks come from the same basin, the product has no complementary expert to
supply the background, and the background is exactly what goes wrong in 5 of 5 slots. The first pick
looking like a clean instance of the composition and the composition not depending on it are the
same observation from two sides.)*

Two consequences:

* **The worry that prompted this — "are the prototypes just the query?" — is answered NO**, on top of
  §5e-quinquies' separate finding that they are not memorized held-out images either.
* **Why `poe-guided` ≈ `query-only` on three backbones — answered below.**

#### The guidance channel is the bottleneck, and it has no usable setting

The two teachers are genuinely different: their means sit **0.082 apart** in RMSE, while
`query_only_teacher`'s mean *is* the query. So the question is whether Eq. 13 transmits that
separation. `tests/diag_poe_vs_queryonly.py` sweeps w₀ and measures what arrives — three OOD slots,
12 samples each, `d(samples)/d(means)` being the fraction of the teacher difference that survives:

| w₀ | d(means) | d(samples) | **transmitted** | joint, PoE | joint, query-only |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.5 | 0.0820 | 0.0261 | **0.32** | 0.06 | 0.06 |
| **1.2 — the paper's** | 0.0820 | 0.0269 | **0.33** | **0.56** | **0.64** |
| 3.0 | 0.0820 | 0.1470 | 1.79 | **0.00** | **0.00** |
| 8.0 | 0.0820 | 0.1697 | 2.07 | **0.00** | **0.00** |

**At the paper's own w₀ only a third of the difference between the two teachers reaches the
images.** Turning the guidance up does transmit it — the ratio passes 1.0 by w₀ = 3 — but the
composition is gone by then: joint accuracy falls from 0.56 to **0.00** for *both* arms, because
guidance that strong drives the trajectory off the manifold.

**There is no setting where the arms are distinguishable and the composition survives.** That is the
whole explanation, it is a property of Eq. 13/14 rather than a defect in our reimplementation, and it
holds on every backbone we have run. Two teachers 0.08 apart are simply below the resolution of a
channel whose usable dynamic range ends before it can express them.

This is also the quantitative case for Stage 4 of `figures/01_architecture.png`. If the guidance
channel cannot carry the difference between two teacher means, then *no* choice of teacher will
distinguish itself through it — which is why our route stops using the teacher as the sampling
distribution and reads the composition into `c_new` for the model's own conditional to generate.
Note also that at w₀ = 1.2 `query-only` is slightly *ahead* on joint (0.64 vs 0.56): the discovered
composition buys nothing over the query once it has to pass through this channel.

This is **not** memorization of held-out data (§5e-quinquies below). It is discovery selecting its
own initialization.

**It is inherited, not introduced.** Their Table 6 CelebA column specifies query-centred
initialization and their Eq. 8–9 is this objective. The prediction that follows is that their
`query-only` baseline should be strong wherever this bites — and their **CelebA Table 2 has
query-only beating PoE on Faithfulness (F1 64.0 vs 20.3)**, exactly as we observe, while their
ColorMNIST Table 1 puts it at the floor (0.0 vs 75.3). The two rows of their own paper disagree about
this baseline in the same direction our measurements do (see `docs/SETTINGS.md §4c`).

**Confirmed a third time, on their own backbone.** N48 runs this on the N47 fine-tune of
`google/ddpm-celebahq-256` at 128 px — their Table 5 configuration, not ours — with 16 queries pooled
per class and both reference sets. The two arms are again indistinguishable, and our route is not:

| arm (CelebA, paper backbone) | mean attr | joint | General. precision | General. recall |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `poe-guided` | 0.863 | 0.500 | 0.80 | **0.02** |
| `query-only` | **0.865** | **0.500** | 0.73 | **0.03** |
| `ours (ADDRESS)` | 0.817 | 0.323 | 0.61 | **0.36** |
| *unconditional (floor)* | *0.699* | *0.120* | *0.44* | *0.47* |

*(Caveat on the floor row, and it is ours: the `unconditional` arm takes no class input, so at a
fixed seed it drew the **same images for every class** in this run — classes 7 and 12 both scored
attr 0.699 / joint 0.120, differing only in the reference-dependent precision and recall. Read it as
one fixed draw scored per class, not as six independent floors. `n48` now offsets the seed per class;
the run above predates that.)*

### 5e-sexies. Their Table 2 reproduced on their own backbone (N48, corrected 2026-08-13)

> **This section replaces a retracted one.** Its first version concluded "our arm does not clear the
> unconditional floor on CelebA" and scoped the composition claim to 3-factor benchmarks. That was
> measured through an indexing bug in `attr_accuracy` (the target combination was indexed by position
> in a *filtered* attribute list rather than canonical position), which suppressed every arm's
> accuracy — `ours` read 0.131 against a floor of 0.128. Re-run against the fix, the accuracy axis
> reverses completely. The coverage axis never touched the probes and is unchanged.

6 held-out CelebA classes on the N47 fine-tune of `google/ddpm-celebahq-256` at 128 px — their own
Table 5 configuration — 16 queries pooled per class, Inception-V3 for the k-NN metrics, both of their
reference sets.

| arm | mean attr | **joint** | Faithful. P / R / F1 | General. P / R / F1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `poe-guided` | 0.885 | **0.544** | 0.916 / 0.104 / **0.187** | **0.753** / 0.034 / 0.065 |
| `query-only` | 0.879 | 0.528 | 0.898 / 0.177 / 0.296 | 0.681 / 0.039 / 0.074 |
| **`ours (ADDRESS)`** | 0.858 | **0.439** | 0.681 / 0.396 / **0.501** | 0.585 / **0.305** / 0.401 |
| *unconditional (floor)* | *0.733* | *0.161* | *0.635 / 0.396 / 0.488* | *0.474 / 0.359 / **0.409*** |
| **their Table 2, PoE** | — | — | 0.758 / 0.120 / **0.203** | ***0.745*** / 0.090 / 0.156 |

**Their method reproduces.** PoE Faithfulness F1 **0.187 against their published 0.203**, and
Generalization *precision* **0.753 against their 0.745** — within 0.008, through an independent
reimplementation on a separately fine-tuned backbone. Unchanged by the bug fix, because the
precision/recall columns are Inception-space quantities that never touch the attribute probes.

**Our arm clears the floor on the axis that was corrupted.** Joint accuracy **0.439 against 0.161**,
a 2.7× margin, and it holds on every class individually:

| class | 7 | 9 | 12 | 14 | 15 | 25 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `ours` joint | 0.323 | 0.432 | 0.609 | 0.427 | 0.375 | 0.469 |
| floor | 0.120 | 0.083 | 0.260 | 0.068 | 0.214 | 0.224 |
| ratio | 2.7× | **5.2×** | 2.3× | **6.3×** | 1.8× | 2.1× |

**And the accuracy/coverage scissors from ColorMNIST appears here too.** Read the `joint` and
Generalization-recall columns together, because neither alone ranks the arms:

* `poe-guided` and `query-only` are the most *accurate* (0.544, 0.528) and cover almost nothing
  (recall 0.034, 0.039) — they re-emit the query, exactly as on ColorMNIST and as their own §4.3
  describes for query-only.
* `unconditional` covers the most (recall 0.359) and carries the composition worst (joint 0.161) —
  a diffuse sampler reaches the class manifold without ever being asked to.
* **`ours` is the only arm on both axes**: accuracy 2.7× the floor *and* recall 9× `poe-guided`'s.

**What does not clear the floor, and is not corrected.** Our Generalization **F1** is 0.401 against
the floor's 0.409 — a tie, because F1 mixes precision with recall and an unconstrained sampler buys
recall by being diffuse. That number was never affected by the bug and it stands: on coverage-F1
alone, ADDRESS is not distinguishable from not conditioning at all. The claim the data supports is
the joint one — coverage *at* accuracy — not coverage by itself.

**Both probe spaces, and they agree on the ratio.** `joint` requires every readable attribute
simultaneously, so it is sensitive to how the probes were fit. The run was done twice -- probes on
raw pixels (4-5 readable of 14) and on Inception-V3 pool3 features (6 of 14) -- and the absolute
levels move a long way while the comparison does not:

| | ours `joint` | floor `joint` | ratio | per-class ratios |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| pixel probes | 0.439 | 0.161 | **2.7x** | 1.8 / 2.1 / 2.3 / 2.7 / 5.2 / 6.3 |
| Inception probes | 0.245 | 0.108 | **2.3x** | 1.6 / 2.1 / 2.4 / 5.0 / 6.0 / floor 0.000 |

The Inception run is the stricter of the two -- six attributes must all be right at once instead of
five -- which is why every level is lower, and it is also the one that does not depend on `Bald`
(base rate 0.000 in this split, so a probe that cannot be wrong; `probe_usable` now rejects it). The
margin over the floor survives both, on every class in both. The coverage columns are identical
between the runs to within 0.003, as they must be: precision and recall are Inception-space
quantities in both cases and never touch the attribute probes.

### 5e-quinquies. Are the recovered prototypes memorized held-out images? No — the split is clean

Prompted by a reading of the case studies: several prototypes look like clean instances of the OOD
composition, which would be retrieval wearing composition's clothes if the backbone had ever seen
them. `tests/diag_holdout_leak.py` checks both halves of that.

**The split, asserted against the checkpoint rather than assumed.** `n1_train` fits on
`data["X"][seen]` and remaps only the seen slots; the audit confirms it on the artifact itself:

| | |
|---|---|
| slots | **120 seen, 40 held out** |
| slots with a row in the checkpoint's class table | **120** |
| held-out slots with a trained embedding row | **0** |
| trained rows that are not seen slots | **0** |

**The prototypes, by nearest neighbour.** For each *selected* prototype, distance to the query, to
other images of the same OOD slot (never seen), and to the training set — with the median distance
between two real held-out images of that slot as the scale bar. Memorization would mean a prototype
lands closer to an unseen image than real images of that slot land to each other, while sitting far
from anything trained on.

**It never happens: 24 of 24 prototypes** (4 slots × 2 initializations × K = 3) are nearest to the
**training** set. Not one is nearest to the held-out set.

| slot | init | k | d(query) | d(held-out) | d(train) | *real-real* |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 46 | query | 0 | 0.1148 | 0.1524 | **0.1176** | *0.1255* |
| 46 | **random** | 0 | 0.1361 | 0.1013 | **0.0691** | *0.1255* |
| 110 | query | 0 | 0.1252 | 0.1159 | **0.1074** | *0.1046* |
| 110 | **random** | 0 | 0.1718 | 0.1192 | **0.0890** | *0.1046* |
| 19 | **random** | 0 | 0.1162 | 0.0931 | **0.0871** | *0.0523* |
| 99 | query | 0 | 0.1057 | 0.1130 | **0.0744** | *0.1043* |
| 99 | **random** | 0 | 0.1683 | 0.1362 | **0.0855** | *0.1043* |

The **random-initialized** rows carry the weight: those chains start from noise and never see the
query, so "it resembles the query because it started there" cannot explain them — and they land
*closest of all* to the training set (0.069–0.089, in several cases well under the intra-class
spread). That is what a mode of the trained distribution looks like.

**So the resemblance is real and innocent, for two reasons.** ColorMNIST is factored: a held-out slot
shares its digit with seen slots and both its colours with other seen slots, so a mode carrying the
right digit in the right colours can exist without any held-out image ever having been shown — that
is the phenomenon under study, not a leak. And the first-picked prototype additionally sits near the
query because selection is scored *on the query* (§5e-quater).

**What this does not license.** It clears memorization of held-out *images*. It says nothing about
whether the composition is genuinely discovered rather than inherited from the query, which is
§5e-quater's separate and less comfortable finding.

#### The specific reading that keeps prompting this, and the direct answer to it

The figure invites it: in `figures/case_colormnist.png` the **first** of the three prototypes is a
clean instance of the held-out composition while the other two look like noise, which reads as the
model having been shown that class. It was not, and `tests/diag_prototype_origin.py` settles it on
the exact compositions the figure renders.

**First, at the data level** — the four shown slots, checked against the checkpoint's own remap:

| composition | slot | rows in TRAIN | class-embedding row |
|---|---|---|---|
| digit 9, cyan on deep_red | 152 | **0** | **none** |
| digit 4, green on navy | 69 | **0** | **none** |
| digit 7, pink on dark_purple | 126 | **0** | **none** |
| digit 1, yellow on dark_brown | 19 | **0** | **none** |

**Second, the resemblance is the initialization**, and the provenance now proves it rather than
suggesting it. `meta["src"]` records where each candidate's chain began:

| composition | pick | chain began | u | d(query) | d(train) | in class-spreads |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 9, cyan/deep_red | **0** | **query** | 0.050 | 0.1319 | 0.1597 | 0.76 |
| | 1 | random | 0.200 | 0.2484 | 0.1820 | 1.43 |
| 4, green/navy | **0** | **query** | 0.050 | 0.1144 | **0.0952** | 0.94 |
| 7, pink/dark_purple | **0** | **query** | 0.075 | 0.1326 | 0.1312 | 0.90 |
| 1, yellow/dark_brown | **0** | **query** | 0.050 | 0.1106 | **0.0809** | 1.01 |

**Pick 0 is the query-seeded chain at the lowest noise level, in every block**, and picks 1–2 are
random-seeded at higher noise — which is exactly why they look unrelated. Selection scores candidates
on the query (Eq. 8–9), so the query-seeded one is always chosen first.

**Third, it is not the query re-displayed.** Pick 0 sits **0.76–1.01 class-spreads** from the query —
a full class-width of movement — and in two of four blocks it ends up *closer to the training set
than to the query it started from* (0.0952 vs 0.1144; 0.0809 vs 0.1106). It is a mode of the trained
distribution reached from the query, which is what compositional generalization looks like when the
digit, the foreground and the background are each trained and only their conjunction is new.

And it is not what carries the result: excluding query-seeded candidates from selection leaves the
composition identical in 6/6 slots (§5e-quater). The figure now labels each prototype with its
provenance and noise level, because hiding that asymmetry is what made the panel misread twice.

**What did not reproduce, and it is downstream of the row above.** `+LoRA` — their distillation,
which their Table 1 reports as their **best** arm (F1 87.6 / 76.3) — comes out at F1 0.169 / 0.005
here, with joint accuracy 0.005. It is not an independent failure: its **Vendi score is 1.15**, i.e.
roughly *one effective sample* out of 64, against 2.17 for `ours` on the identical budget. The
adapter is distilled on 256 images drawn from the PoE teacher, and that pool is itself collapsed
(vendi 1.06 from 24 draws, `tests/diag_teacher_variance.py`). Distilling 256 near-copies of one image
teaches one image, and the arm cannot exceed the pool it learns from.

So `+LoRA` inherits the `poe-guided` degeneracy rather than adding a new one, and fixing that would
have to come first. Recorded as a **failed reproduction with an identified cause**, not as a negative
result about their method — on their own numbers the distilled arm *improves* on their PoE
(88.0 vs 80.7 recall), which is exactly what a healthy pool would predict.

### 30b. The pretrained backbone was being fed scrambled input, and it invalidates the whole pretrained track

This is the more serious half, and it is the blocker for replicating the companion paper at all,
because **their entire CelebA setup is a fine-tune of `google/ddpm-celebahq-256`.**

On a backbone we train ourselves, the permutation costs the convolutional prior — bad, but the model
at least learns *in* that space. On a **pretrained** model it is a different kind of error: the
weights are fixed and were fitted to real images, so a permuted row is simply wrong input to a frozen
function. `tests/diag_pretrained_layout.py` measures it three ways on the untouched model:

| | `permuted` (what the repo did) | `hwc` (a real image) |
|---|---|---|
| one-step denoise of a real face, RMSE | 0.3796 | **0.0763** |
| ... against a no-op baseline of | 0.6048 | 0.6048 |
| unconditional samples | **a 3×3 tiled mosaic of greyscale faces** | photorealistic colour faces |

**Under the permuted layout the field returned a 3×3 tiled greyscale mosaic** while `DDPMPipeline`
sampling the *same weights* returns photorealistic faces (`figures/extra/f25_pretrained_layout.png`). The
one-step denoise recovers the target at RMSE 0.076 under `hwc` against 0.380 permuted — a 5× gap,
and permuted is barely better than the 0.605 you get by doing nothing at all.

![the paper's backbone, permuted vs real input](../figures/extra/f25_pretrained_layout.png)

**Consequences, stated plainly.**

* **Every image-quality number measured through the pretrained field is void**, including §29a's
  finding that the paper's own backbone still produced rough samples (tv 1.45–4.82). That was our
  field being broken, not their backbone. **§29a's conclusion — "the damage is downstream of the
  backbone" — is retracted.**
* **The preservation claims survive**, because bit-identity is a property of optimizer scoping and
  does not depend on what the input tensor looks like. §12's 0.000e+00 preservation still holds.
* The 3×3 mosaic is the same failure mode §5o recorded on our own CelebA backbone in a different
  guise, which is why it read as familiar and was mistaken for "undertrained".

`PretrainedScoreField` now defaults to `layout="hwc"` and converts at all four boundaries — `eps` in
and out, `x_init`, the guidance callback, and the return value. The pretrained track is being rerun.

### 30d-CLOSED. It was the renderer, not the bank (2026-08-12)

**§30d below is resolved, and the answer is that nothing was ever wrong with the adapter.** The
symptom — "rainbow noise with fine horizontal striping" — is what an **HWC-flat row read as
CHW-flat** looks like, and the two conventions in play disagree:

```
n25_pretrained_case.show()   reshape(3, img, img).transpose(1, 2, 0)     <- assumes CHW-flat
PretrainedScoreField         layout="hwc"; _to_flat: permute(0,2,3,1)    <- returns HWC-flat
```

`n26` loads its images CHW-flat and un-flattens them itself for *training*, so training is
self-consistent and the audit below correctly cleared it. But it *samples* through `field.sample()`,
which returns rows in the **field's** layout, and then renders them with `show`. Everything after the
sampler was transposed.

`tests/diag_pretrained_render.py` settles it with no adapter and no consolidation at all — the
**untouched** backbone, four samples, read both ways (`figures/extra/f30_pretrained_render.png`):

| read | vertical/horizontal gradient ratio |
|---|---|
| real CelebA photographs (the bar) | 0.876 |
| **CHW — what `show` does** | **2.080** |
| HWC — what the field returns | **0.963** |

The CHW row is the reported failure, pixel for pixel, produced by a model with **nothing attached to
it**. The HWC row is photorealistic faces. Same samples.

**Why every previous hypothesis missed it.** The controlled sweep measured `tv` on the flat arrays,
and the control was transposed identically — so the *ratio* stayed near 1 while both pictures were
scrambled. A metric that cannot see the defect reported no defect, three times over.

This is the same fault as §30b (the model's **input** being permuted) on the model's **output**, and
it is the fourth instance in this repository of a layout convention crossing a boundary
undeclared. The lasting fix is that flat rows should never be passed between a field and a renderer
without the layout travelling with them.

**What this does and does not restore.** It removes the last unexplained failure from the pretrained
track, and it means N26's attribute/vendi numbers were computed on correctly-shaped arrays all
along — only the figure was wrong. It does **not** revive N25's retracted composition result: that
was measured through a field feeding the UNet a permuted **input** (§30b), which is a different bug
and a real one.

### 30d. Open: the adapter bank still produces noise on the pretrained backbone (N26) — *superseded by 30d-CLOSED above; kept for the record of what was ruled out*

Not everything downstream of the layout fix recovered. `n26_batch_pretrained` consolidates four
attribute concepts into `google/ddpm-celebahq-256` through the LoRA bank, and its samples are
**rainbow noise with fine horizontal striping** — before the layout work and after it, pixel for
pixel the same failure (`figures/extra/n26_batch_pretrained.png`).

**It is not the layout, and the check is direct.** n26 carries its own CHW-flat convention
end to end: it loads images with `.transpose(2,0,1).ravel()` and its own `train_group` un-flattens
them with `.reshape(-1, 3, img, img)`, which is a true image. Training and sampling therefore agree,
and re-running after the consolidation paths were made layout-aware produced an identical figure.

What the numbers say about where to look next:

| arm | attr | vendi | preservation | params |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| sequential | 0.67 | 1.55 | **exact 1.00** | 0.98 M |
| batch | 0.58 | 1.53 | **exact 1.00** | 0.98 M |
| batch-shared | 0.50 | 1.57 | **exact 1.00** | 1.18 M |

The adapter reaches a training loss of **0.0002** — it is fitting its pool almost exactly — while
preservation stays bit-identical, which is consistent and unhelpful: bit-identity is a property of
optimizer scoping and cannot detect that the delta being learned is destructive.

**What a controlled sweep rules out** (`tests/diag_pretrained_adapter.py`). One concept, real images,
the untouched backbone as control at tv **0.973**:

| setting | tv | final loss |
|---|---|---|
| rank 8, lr 1e-3, 40 steps *(n26's hyperparameters)* | 0.770 | 0.0004 |
| rank 8, lr 1e-3, 200 steps | 0.686 | 0.0003 |
| rank 8, lr 1e-3, 600 steps *(≈ n26's full budget)* | **0.919** | 0.0002 |
| rank 8, lr 1e-4 / 1e-5, 40 steps | 0.978 / 0.973 | — |
| rank 1, lr 1e-3, 40 steps | 1.160 | — |

**None of them destroys the model.** At n26's own rank, learning rate and optimization budget the
samples stay near the control, so *the perturbation magnitude is not the fault* — which was the
leading hypothesis and is now dead. Two others died with it: the pool is real CelebA photographs,
not generated, so "it faithfully learned a bad pool" was never available; and `add_concept`'s `mu`
argument feeds **tree placement**, not conditioning, so passing a pool mean there is correct rather
than a dimension error.

So the failure is specific to something in `n26`'s own setup that this sweep does not reproduce.
The remaining candidates, none yet tested: the four-concept batch rather than one; `train_group`
zeroing and excluding the concept embedding so identity rests entirely on the LoRA path; or the
rendering call itself. **This is recorded as open, not as diagnosed** — three plausible explanations
have already died here and a fourth guess would not be worth more than the three.

**This is the honest state of "our method on a backbone we did not train" for image quality.** §12's
preservation result stands — it never depended on the samples looking right — but the pretrained bank
is not currently producing usable images, and no claim in this document should be read as saying it
is.

### 30e. The CelebA panel was comparing generations against images that produced none of them

Looking again at `f18_celeba_paper_paperdisc_w05.png` after §30c's fixes, two things still read as
broken: the prototypes carry a colour cast (neon magenta in one block, electric blue in another), and
**that cast reappears in the rows beneath them** — including `query-only`, whose teacher is
N(x_q, σ²I) and which never sees a prototype. The obvious story is that bad prototypes contaminate
the teacher mean. `query-only` refutes it. The next obvious story is that all arms in a block share
the seed, and their DDIM at η=0 is deterministic, so the seed decides the image. That is also wrong.

`tests/diag_celeba_cast.py` separates the two by varying one thing at a time:

| | mean RGB | spread across the varied axis |
|---|---|---|
| **A. one query, six seeds** | [0.637, 0.549, 0.490] | **0.0154** |
| **B. six queries, one seed** | [0.523, 0.447, 0.379] | **0.1485** |
| *the six real queries themselves* | *[0.547, 0.460, 0.403]* | *0.1549* |

**The cast tracks the query, not the seed** — B's spread is 0.1485 against the queries' own 0.1549,
and its mean colour sits within 0.024 of theirs on every channel, while changing the seed moves the
colour by 0.015. `query-only` is doing exactly what a Gaussian centred on x_q should do: reproducing
the query's colour faithfully.

**So why did it look wrong?** Because the figure was not showing the query. `n44` drew the query at a
*random* index of the held-out combo (`q = idx[rng.integers(len(idx))]`) and rendered the row labelled
"real query" from a *different* set — `idx[:n_show]`, the first six images of that combo. The two
coincided only by chance. Every reader of that panel, this one included, was invited to compare
generations against images that had driven none of them, and a cast faithful to an unshown image
reads as an artifact. Fixed: the query is now column 1 of that row and the label says so.

**What remains wrong with the prototypes is smaller and more specific than it looks.** Part C of the
same diagnostic measures each prototype's mean colour against its query's:

| case | query RGB | prototype RGB | \|ΔRGB\| | fraction outside [−1,1] |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | [0.648, 0.547, 0.509] | [0.676, 0.562, 0.481] | **0.024** | 4.4 % |
| 1 | [0.726, 0.656, 0.587] | [0.760, 0.674, 0.538] | **0.033** | **18.4 %** |
| 2 | [0.651, 0.525, 0.461] | [0.695, 0.528, 0.431] | **0.026** | 1.7 % |

The prototypes are *not* globally mis-coloured — their average colour sits within 0.033 of the
query's on every case, as close as `query-only`'s samples get. The neon is **localized**: between
1.7 % and 18.4 % of coordinates driven to the boundary, which clipping renders as flat patches of
pure colour, and the eye reads a patch as a cast over the whole image. That is §30c's finding — a
defect in mode ascent, not in the composition — and the per-case spread shows it is query-dependent
rather than a constant property of the settings.

What this section clears is the *sampling* path: the generations are faithful to their query, and the
apparent contamination of one row by another was an artifact of the figure's own indexing. The
regenerated panel shows it directly — the block whose samples are all electric blue has a **query
that is itself a blue-lit photograph**, and the block whose samples sit on red has a query shot
against red. Both were previously represented by six ordinary portraits of the same attribute
combination, so the fidelity looked like a defect.

**What is still visibly wrong, and is now isolated.** In each block one of the three selected
prototypes is saturated while the other two are clean faces. `candidates_ascent` pools ascent output
over the whole u-grid (CelebA: 0.05 … 0.50) and submodular selection then draws K = 3 from that
pool, and `tests/diag_prototype_quality.py` measured the upper end of that grid returning junk — so
the hypothesis is that the bad prototype is simply the high-noise one, and a pool that is not
filtered by level lets it be selected. `n44` now prints the noise level and out-of-gamut fraction of
each selected mode, which decides that rather than leaving it to be read off a picture.

### 30c. Saturated prototypes: Adam was finding the boundary, not a mode

With the layout fixed the pretrained CelebA samples became real faces, which exposed the fault
underneath: the prototypes came out **neon posterized** -- flat regions of pure saturated colour with
the face structure faintly visible through them.

That signature is specific. It is not "slightly out of range": it is a large fraction of coordinates
sitting at *exactly* the boundary, which is what clipping does to values driven past it. Adding a
range clamp confirmed the reading by failing in an informative way — it changed the numbers and left
the pictures identical, because the values still arrived at the edge and merely stopped there.

**The cause is the optimizer.** `mode_ascent` used **Adam**, whose update is normalized: every
coordinate moves by about `lr` per iteration however small its gradient is. Near a mode the gradient
vanishes and a plain gradient step vanishes with it — Adam's does not. It keeps taking full-sized
steps and marches past. Measured on the pretrained CelebA backbone at u = 0.15, with the query's own
eps error (**0.0260**) as the bar, since eps error is the quantity ascent exists to reduce:

| setting | fraction of x₀ outside [−1,1] | eps error at the returned point |
|---|---|---|
| Adam, lr 0.09, 100 it *(their Table 6)* | **11.1 %** | 0.0113 |
| Adam, lr 0.02, 100 it | 0.0 % | 0.0056 |
| Adam, lr 0.09, 20 it | 0.3 % | 0.0391 — *worse than the query* |
| **plain, lr 0.09, 100 it** | **0.0 %** | **0.0046** |
| plain, lr 1.00, 100 it | 32.9 % | 0.0469 — *worse than the query* |

**Plain ascent wins on both axes** — entirely in gamut *and* a lower-error mode than Adam finds. It
is now the default, recorded in `docs/SETTINGS.md §5c` as a measured deviation from their Table 6,
with `adam=True` retained to reproduce their column.

**Final numbers, under the single global setting** (the paper's Adam, no clamp, no Tweedie; their
Table 8 `w0 = 0.5`; input layout corrected), on `google/ddpm-celebahq-256`:

| arm | before any fix | after |
|---|---|---|
| prototypes (mode ascent) | 0.78 – 1.40 | 0.54 – 0.59 |
| `poe-guided` | **2.50 – 4.82** | **0.89 – 1.23** |
| `query-only` | 3.53 – 4.27 | 0.93 – 1.19 |

`poe-guided` renders at real-image texture on the paper's own backbone. Note these are the numbers
with **Adam restored** — they are indistinguishable from the ones the ascent changes produced
(0.82–1.11), which is the evidence that those changes were never doing the work.

![the paper's pipeline on its own backbone, after the layout, guidance and ascent fixes](../figures/extra/f18_celeba_paper_paperdisc_w05.png)

**Four explanations were tried and three were refuted by measurement**, which is worth recording
because each was plausible: runaway Adam steps (max |x₀| measured 1.5–2.0, not the 9 predicted), the
x₀ estimator (un-clipped Tweedie measured *identical* to the old rescaling), and the clamp itself.
The one that held is the one adjudicated by the objective rather than by argument.

### 30a. The CelebA banding artifact IS the permutation — confirmed with a control and a counter-control

The samples show horizontal seams and rectangular patches cutting an otherwise coherent face. The
permuted layout predicts exactly that, and predicts **where**: its three "channels" are image rows
[0, H/3), [H/3, 2H/3), [2H/3, H), the first convolution treats channels independently, and nothing
enforces continuity where the bands meet. For 64px that is **rows 21 and 43**.

`tests/diag_band_artifact.py` scores the row-to-row difference at those two boundaries against the
median elsewhere. 1.0 means no seam:

| | seam score at rows 21 / 43 |
|---|---|
| **real CelebA** (control) | **0.906** — no seam |
| our 64px backbone, `permuted` | **2.314** |
| 12-epoch matched pair, `permuted` | 1.424 |
| 12-epoch matched pair, **`hwc`** (counter-control) | **1.004** — seam gone |

![where CelebA samples discontinue](../figures/extra/f24_band_artifact.png)

**The prediction was sharp and it held.** The discontinuity spikes at precisely the two rows the
permutation implies, at 2.6× the level real data shows, and an otherwise identical backbone trained
in the correct layout sits at the control's own value. This is the artifact, it is the layout, and it
is fixable.

It also explains why §30's accuracy test missed it. Joint composition accuracy asks whether the digit
and the colours are right; it is indifferent to a seam across the face. **The layout fix should be
judged on artifacts and on the training objective — where it wins decisively — not on ColorMNIST
composition accuracy, where it does not.** §30's paired result stands; it was measuring the wrong
thing for this question.

**What this does and does not touch.** The lifelong machinery is unaffected: it consolidates
faithfully whatever it is given (98% retention, bit-identical preservation). What is undermined is
the *value* of consolidating, since a collapsed pool teaches an adapter one image. Composition gets
the concept's **location** right and its **extent** wrong, and extent is what a generative concept
needs.

## 5f. N7 — conditioning the manifold instead of guiding it (the fix)

The architecture: **the manifold follows the teacher, and sampling follows the manifold.** The PoE
teacher locates the concept and never generates; the model's own class conditional — the part
measured at the ceiling — does the generating. A concept becomes a point in the model's conditioning
space, learned by textual inversion restricted to the span of the trained class embeddings,
`c_new = Σ_k α_k c_k` (`lifelong_ood/neural/inversion.py`).

Restricting to that span does three things at once: ~120 parameters cannot memorize one 3072-dim
image; every point in the span is known to decode to a diverse conditional, so diversity is inherited
rather than hoped for; and `α` is itself a composition statement — which seen compositions the unseen
one is built from, in the model's own vocabulary.

8 held-out compositions, 32 samples each, scored with the paper's metric set:

| arm | acc | digit | fg | bg | precision | **recall** | **F1** | content div ÷ real |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `teacher-guided` (what we shipped) | **0.58** | 0.80 | 0.90 | **0.84** | 0.01 | 0.03 | **0.00** | 0.74 |
| `inversion` | 0.32 | 0.78 | 0.94 | 0.48 | 0.33 | 0.85 | 0.42 | 1.16 |
| **`inversion+init`** (teacher-seeded) | 0.29 | 0.66 | 0.83 | 0.60 | **0.39** | 0.83 | **0.45** | 1.21 |
| `inversion@pool` | 0.01 | 0.15 | 0.80 | 0.26 | 0.02 | 0.19 | 0.02 | 1.09 |
| `nearest-class` | 0.00 | 0.14 | 0.49 | 0.00 | 0.00 | 0.27 | 0.00 | 1.14 |
| *ceiling: base model on a SEEN class* | *0.97* | | | | *0.97* | *0.95* | *0.96* | *1.01* |

- **F1 goes 0.00 → 0.48 and recall 0.02 → 0.88**, with content diversity restored from 0.72 to 1.16×
  the real class. The coverage failure that survived every guidance, covariance, pooling and
  stochasticity change is fixed by changing *what generates* rather than *how it is steered*.
- **Teacher seeding helps** (`inversion+init` beats `inversion` on precision 0.39 vs 0.33 and F1
  0.45 vs 0.42), which is the "manifold follows teacher" half doing work.
- **`nearest-class` is not a substitute** (F1 0.00): conditioning on the single best trained class
  does not recover the composition, so the learned α is doing something a lookup cannot.
- **Using the teacher's POOL as the inversion target fails** (recall 0.19). The pool is collapsed, so
  inverting on it teaches the embedding one image — the same garbage-in problem that stopped
  distillation from rescuing diversity in §5e. **The teacher has to constrain through α, not through
  pixels.**

### Certified over 3 seeds

The arms below are now pooled over 3 seeds (mean ± SE, `experiments/aggregate.py`), 8 held-out
compositions each. **Every fidelity variant I tried — region-weighted loss, inverting on μ_T,
3× longer optimization, CFG on the learned embedding — falls inside the error bars of plain
inversion or below it.** Those were being tuned on single-seed noise; plain inversion is the method.

| arm (3 seeds) | acc | precision | **recall** | **F1** | content div ÷ real |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `teacher-guided` | **0.585 ± 0.081** | 0.040 ± 0.020 | 0.062 ± 0.026 | **0.003 ± 0.003** | 0.774 ± 0.032 |
| **`inversion`** | 0.234 ± 0.040 | **0.284 ± 0.068** | **0.649 ± 0.066** | **0.323 ± 0.057** | **1.166 ± 0.033** |
| `inv` + region-weighted loss | 0.142 ± 0.006 | 0.186 ± 0.027 | 0.605 ± 0.085 | 0.252 ± 0.031 | 1.176 ± 0.011 |
| `inv` on μ_T | 0.217 ± 0.053 | 0.268 ± 0.014 | 0.612 ± 0.024 | 0.291 ± 0.050 | 1.133 ± 0.047 |
| `inv` 3× longer | 0.174 ± 0.050 | 0.219 ± 0.026 | 0.542 ± 0.158 | 0.232 ± 0.060 | 1.152 ± 0.054 |
| `nearest-class` | 0.000 ± 0.000 | 0.004 ± 0.004 | 0.069 ± 0.039 | 0.002 ± 0.002 | 1.063 ± 0.050 |
| *ceiling: base model, SEEN class* | *0.97* | *0.97* | *0.95* | *0.96* | *1.01* |

**F1 0.003 ± 0.003 → 0.323 ± 0.057** and **recall 0.062 → 0.649** are separated by many standard
errors — this is the one result here that is unambiguously certified. **Content diversity reaches
1.166 ± 0.033 against a target of 1.0**, i.e. the samples now vary about as much as the real class
does. The cost is equally certain: **accuracy 0.585 → 0.234**, concentrated in background.

**Final numbers, 8 held-out compositions (single seed, for the per-arm detail):**

| arm | acc | digit | fg | bg | precision | **recall** | **F1** | content div ÷ real |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `teacher-guided` (what we shipped) | **0.56** | 0.80 | 0.93 | **0.76** | 0.00 | 0.02 | **0.00** | 0.72 |
| **`inversion`** | 0.33 | 0.65 | 0.96 | 0.56 | **0.42** | **0.88** | **0.48** | 1.16 |
| `inversion+init` (teacher-seeded) | 0.25 | 0.61 | 0.85 | 0.56 | 0.32 | 0.64 | 0.36 | 1.15 |
| `inversion+anchor` (teacher as prior) | 0.00 | 0.12 | 0.26 | 0.34 | 0.00 | 0.63 | 0.01 | 1.34 |
| `nearest-class` | 0.00 | 0.25 | 0.50 | 0.00 | 0.01 | 0.07 | 0.01 | 1.15 |
| *ceiling: base model, SEEN class* | *0.97* | | | | *0.97* | *0.95* | *0.96* | *1.01* |

**F1 0.00 → 0.48, recall 0.02 → 0.88.**

**Two things that did not work, and one that reframes the architecture.**

*The anchor is a dead end.* Penalizing `‖α − α_teacher‖²` collapses the concept in every
configuration tried (F1 0.01–0.03, accuracy 0.00) regardless of anchor strength or which prior is
anchored to. Diagnosing why produced something more useful than the fix would have been — see below.

*The teacher's prior over classes was measurably broken, then measurably fixed, and it still did not
help.* Scoring trained classes by ε-loss on μ_T ranks them by foreground and is **below chance on
background** (0.10 against 0.25). The cause is structural: a diffusion model's ε-error concentrates
where there is structure, so a whole-image score is dominated by the stroke. Scoring the foreground
and background regions separately and combining them fixes the coverage — but only as a **mixture**,
never a product:

| prior | mass on fg-constituent | mass on bg-constituent | balance |
|---|---|---|---|
| chance | 0.25 | 0.25 | 1.00 |
| global, whole image | 0.32 | 0.24 | 0.75 |
| per-factor **product** | 0.78 | **0.00** | 0.01 |
| per-factor **mixture** (default) | **0.52** | **0.31** | 0.60 |

The product is empty *by construction*: the benchmark holds out `(fg, bg)` **pairs**, so a class
matching the target's foreground necessarily has a different background and no trained class
satisfies both constraints. A composition in class space has to be a **union of constituents**, each
supplying the factor it owns. That is the exact dual of the pixel-space finding in §5e — the product
is the right operator for *locating* a concept and the wrong one for expressing its *extent*.

*And the teacher turns out to be redundant for this step.* With the improved prior, teacher
initialization **hurts** (F1 0.36 vs 0.48 for free inversion). Plain inversion on the query alone is
the best arm. That is a real dent in the "manifold follows the teacher" story as applied to the
neural conditioning step: the query is itself an image of the target composition, so it already
carries the information the teacher was meant to supply. The teacher still does the discovery, still
produces a μ_T that probe-reads correctly (0.800 against a 0.775 ceiling), and is essential on the
analytic backbone — but for choosing a point in the model's conditioning space it adds nothing
measurable here. Single seed, 8 slots; the earlier run with the weaker prior had init helping
slightly, so the honest statement is that teacher seeding is *not reliably beneficial*, not that it
is harmful.

**The honest remainder.** Fidelity has not caught up: accuracy 0.56 → 0.33, concentrated in
background (0.76 → 0.56) while foreground *improves* (0.93 → 0.96). Neither end is the ceiling
(0.97 acc, 0.96 F1). The mechanisms are cleanly separated now — guidance controlled fidelity and
could not buy coverage; conditioning buys coverage and still needs a fidelity term that anchoring
does not provide.


## 5g. Lifelong with the best composer (N3, 3 seeds)

The full loop with `--concept-mode factored`, so what gets consolidated is a pool drawn from the
model's own conditional at a concept read in the ideal-words basis with the per-factor gain:

| arm (3 seeds, 10 concepts) | bit-identical retention | new-concept accuracy | **pool coverage (recall)** | frozen-base drift |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `hier` | **1.000 ± 0.000** | 0.358 ± 0.027 | **0.750 ± 0.032** | **0.00000 ± 0.00000** |
| `unfrozen` | 1.000 ± 0.000 | 0.342 ± 0.022 | 0.723 ± 0.030 | 0.00000 ± 0.00000 |

**The composer improvements carry into the lifelong loop.** Against the same experiment run with the
class-basis inversion, pool coverage rises **0.411 ± 0.059 → 0.750 ± 0.032** and new-concept accuracy
**0.258 ± 0.030 → 0.358 ± 0.027**, while retention and base drift stay exactly where they were. That
is the division of labour working as intended: fixing *what* is composed and *what generates* changed
the quality of what enters the manifold, and changed nothing about the guarantees on entering it.

⚠️ **The `unfrozen` anomaly persists and remains unexplained.** It scores the same retention as
`hier` here, though the concepts do share ancestors and `tests/test_neural.py` still asserts and
passes the perturbation property on synthetic data. The plausible cause is visible in the
distillation losses — pools drawn from the model's own conditional start at ~0.02–0.03, so gradients
into shared ancestors are tiny — but this is **not verified**. The no-forgetting claim rests on N5
(§5k), where the controls separate cleanly and by large margins.

## 5h. The fidelity limit is architectural, not a tuning failure (the oracle)

Thirteen routes were tried to recover composition fidelity under conditioning, and all failed:
guidance schedules (7), teacher covariance (product / mixture / 3 scales), pooling across queries,
ancestral sampling, distillation, anchoring α to the teacher, a region-weighted inversion loss,
inverting on μ_T, 3× longer optimization, CFG on the learned embedding, per-region composition of
the model's conditionals, and nearest-class. Background never moved off 0.39–0.55.

So rather than try a fourteenth, **measure the ceiling**: fit α on **64 real held-out images of the
target slot** — data the method never receives — and see what the span itself can do.

| | acc | digit | fg | bg | recall | F1 | content div ÷ real |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| inversion from ONE query | 0.23 | 0.63 | 0.81 | 0.49 | 0.65 | 0.32 | 1.17 |
| **ORACLE: α fitted on 64 REAL images** | **0.19** | 0.73 | 0.42 | 0.82 | 0.64 | 0.31 | 1.09 |
| base model on a SEEN class | 0.97 | 0.97 | 1.00 | 1.00 | 0.95 | 0.96 | 1.01 |

**The oracle is no better than inverting from a single query.** Given perfect data it still cannot
place the composition. And the per-factor breakdown shows why in the sharpest possible way: the
oracle gets **background right (0.82) and foreground wrong (0.42)**, while single-query inversion
gets **foreground right (0.81) and background wrong (0.49)**. The span can express one factor or the
other — never both at once.

**Conclusion: no combination of this model's trained class embeddings represents these held-out
(fg, bg) pairings.** The limit is the conditioning space, not the optimizer, the objective, the
prior or the sampler — which is why thirteen interventions on those could not move it. This is the
paper's own stated limitation in a measurable form: *"can compose new primitives that interpolate
between seen ones, but cannot fabricate primitives that extrapolate beyond the training support"*
(their Appendix H).

### What that leaves: two certified operating points, and the real fix

| | composition fidelity | coverage / diversity |
|---|---|---|
| **teacher-guided** (guide toward the PoE Gaussian) | **acc 0.585 ± 0.081** | recall 0.062, div 0.77 |
| **inversion** (condition the manifold) | acc 0.234 ± 0.040 | **recall 0.649 ± 0.066, div 1.166 ± 0.033** |
| *base model, seen class* | *0.97* | *0.95 / 1.01* |

Both are certified over 3 seeds and they are complementary, not ordered: use teacher-guided when the
composition must be right, inversion when the pool must cover the class. For the lifelong loop
inversion is the correct default, because a collapsed pool teaches an adapter a single image.

**The real fix is backbone capacity, and §6.5 already pointed at it.** Our UNet is 6.8M parameters
trained on 24k images; the paper's is ~35M on 120k. A conditioning space learned from 5× the data is
the one plausible way to make these combinations representable, and it is the only remaining
hypothesis this investigation has not been able to test on the hardware available.


## 5i. Why background is lost, and the fix the literature points to

The failure has a name in the compositional-diffusion literature: **attribute binding failure /
colour leakage**, whose standard diagnosis is that a model "has not learned to bind attributes to
objects but has merely memorized common object-attribute pairings" [1,2]. That is literally our
architecture -- conditioning is a single global vector added to the timestep embedding, and each of
the 120 class embeddings is free to be an arbitrary point, so a weighted average of them is not a
composition.

Following Trager et al., *Linear Spaces of Meanings* (ICCV 2023) [3], composite-concept embeddings
are often decomposable as a sum of per-factor vectors -- "ideal words". Fitting that on our table:

    c(digit, fg, bg)  ~  mu + D_digit + F_fg + B_bg          **R² = 0.816**

**So held-out combinations ARE expressible** -- as sums of vectors that each appear in training. The
span oracle of §5h failed not because the space cannot represent these pairings but because a free
weighting over 120 *entangled* class vectors can trade one factor against another, and nothing in a
single global conditioning vector stops it.

Composing in the factor basis instead (`lifelong_ood/neural/idealwords.py`):
