modification. This is the part of the method that transfers cleanly, and it is worth separating from
the part that did not.

### 12b. Three faults, in the order they were removed

**1. Writing to the model's clock.** Our concept vector is added to the *time* embedding. On our own
UNet that slot was trained to carry a class signal; on an unconditional model the time embedding
encodes only the noise level, so an offset on it says *you are at a different timestep*. Training it
corrupts denoising globally. Held at zero; the per-concept adapter path carries the concept.

**2. Adapting the clock by a different route.** Restricting adapters to `linear` layers — where the
personalization literature puts them — turned out to mean the opposite of what it sounds like:
**32 of this model's 58 Linear modules are `time_emb_proj`**, plus two in the time embedding's own
MLP. "Adapt the linears" was adapting the noise-level path in 34 places. Excluding it leaves the 24
attention projections.

**3. Teaching it our blur.** Even attention-only, any learning rate that moved the model also
degraded it. The cause is a distribution mismatch that has nothing to do with the method: our images
are CelebA (178px aligned, upsampled to 256) and the model is CelebA-HQ. Training at low noise levels
— where fine texture is decided — teaches it our upsampling artifacts, which is exactly what the
"painterly and patchy" samples were.

### 12c. The fix, and the number

Training only at **high noise levels**, where semantics are decided and texture is not:

| training range | guidance w | `Eyeglasses` rate | Vendi | samples |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| all t | 1 | 0.00 | 1.54 | already degraded |
| all t | 6 | 0.25 | 1.29 | unrecognizable |
| **t ≥ 500** | 1 | 0.00 | **1.47** | **clean** |
| **t ≥ 500** | 3 | **0.75** | **1.48** | artifacts return |
| **t ≥ 700** | 1 | 0.25 | **1.46** | **clean, glasses present** |
| t ≥ 700 | 3 | 0.50 | 1.36 | stylized but coherent |

*(untouched model: Vendi 1.51)*

So an attribute can be added to a pretrained diffusion model through this bank without damaging it,
provided the adaptation is confined to the noise levels at which the attribute actually lives. The
sampling-time amplification is free: the unconditional branch **is** the frozen model with the
adapter path deactivated, so `eps_frozen + w (eps_adapter − eps_frozen)` needs no CFG dropout and no
unconditional token.

### 12d. What this does and does not license

It does **not** restate the out-of-distribution claim. This model's training set is not ours to
control, so we cannot say it never saw a smiling face in glasses. What is controlled is what **our
concepts** saw: concept A is trained only on `(a=1, b=0)` and concept B only on `(b=1, a=0)`, so
their co-occurrence is held out of both by construction, and composing them is a genuine held-out
combination *at the level of the representations this method builds*.

The general lesson is worth stating separately from this project: **when adapting a pretrained
diffusion model on data from a lower-quality distribution than it was trained on, restrict training
to high noise levels.** Otherwise the adapter spends its capacity transferring your artifacts, and
the failure looks like a capacity or a learning-rate problem when it is neither.

### 12e. The case study, and what it says about our composition operator

> **Corrected at 3 seeds.** The table below is seed 0. Replicated over three seeds, the accuracy
> **RETRACTED 2026-08-11 — the numbers in this block were measured through a broken field.**
> The pretrained UNet was being fed a pixel permutation (§30b), so it returned 3x3 tiled
> greyscale mosaics and the attribute probes were scoring those. Re-run with the layout
> corrected, EVERY generated arm scores `both` = **0.000** — score-composed, path-union, each
> single-concept arm, and the untouched pretrained model alike, against a real-image ceiling
> of 1.000. Preservation is unaffected and still exact; see §30d, which records the failure as
> open. The original text is kept below, struck through by this note rather than deleted, so
> the retraction is legible.
>
> advantage of score composition over path union **does not survive**: both reach `both` = 0.417 ±
> 0.083. What survives is a **diversity** advantage — Vendi 1.436 ± 0.077 against 1.280 ± 0.019,
> where the untouched model is 1.456 — and the ColorMNIST evidence in §13b, where path union produces
> nothing at all (0.000 against 0.042, F1 0.039 against 0.490). The recommendation to compose in
> score space stands on those two, not on the single-seed accuracy gap reported below.
>
> Three-seed table:
>
> | arm | Eyeglasses | Smiling | both | Vendi |
> |---|---|---|---|---|
> | *real* | *1.000* | *0.889* | *0.917* | *1.384* |
> | pretrained (untouched) | 0.167 | 0.611 | 0.167 | 1.456 |
> | **`Eyeglasses` alone** | **1.000 ± 0.000** | 0.167 | 0.250 | 1.443 |
> | **`Smiling` alone** | 0.250 | **1.000 ± 0.000** | 0.250 | 1.468 |
> | ours (score-composed) | 0.833 | 0.667 | **0.417 ± 0.083** | **1.436** |
> | ours (path-union) | 0.667 | 0.778 | **0.417 ± 0.083** | 1.280 |
>
> The acquisition result is *stronger* at three seeds than at one: each concept reaches **1.000 ±
> 0.000** on its own attribute, at a Vendi indistinguishable from the untouched model.

`google/ddpm-celebahq-256`, 128 real images per concept, attention-only adapters trained at t ≥ 600,
sampled with w = 2 against the frozen base. `Eyeglasses` and `Smiling` are learned as separate
concepts, each blind to the other (A on `a=1, b=0`, B on `b=1, a=0`), so their co-occurrence is
withheld from both by construction.

| arm | Eyeglasses | Smiling | both | Vendi |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *real* | *1.00* | *1.00* | *1.00* | *1.38* |
| pretrained (untouched) | 0.00 | 0.67 | 0.00 | 1.51 |
| **`Eyeglasses` alone** | **1.00** | 0.50 | 0.50 | **1.58** |
| **`Smiling` alone** | 0.00 | **1.00** | 0.00 | **1.60** |
| composable-diff (score addition) | 0.67 | 0.83 | **0.50** | 1.56 |
| ours (both adapter paths active) | 0.50 | 0.67 | 0.17 | 1.37 |

**Concept acquisition works, cleanly.** Each concept reaches 1.00 on its own attribute at a Vendi
*above* the untouched model's 1.51, and the samples are recognizable, well-formed 256×256 faces. That
is the thing N15 could not do, and it took the three fixes in §12b plus high-noise-only training.

**Our composition operator is the weakest row in the table**, and the reason is structural rather
than incidental. `ours` composes by activating both adapter paths at once, so it *sums two deltas
that were each trained to be applied alone*. Score addition composes the same two concepts by
combining their predictions instead, and gets `both` 0.50 against our 0.17 while keeping Vendi 1.56
against our 1.37.

This is the third independent measurement pointing the same way — §9g, §11c and now here — and it is
the actionable one, because it names a fix rather than a limitation: **a bank of independently
trained adapters should be composed in SCORE space, not by activating several paths simultaneously.**
The LoRA-merging literature exists precisely because summed deltas interfere (ZipLoRA, Orthogonal
Adaptation, Custom Diffusion's constrained merge all address this), and this repository had been
doing the naive thing at the point where it matters most.

Nothing about preservation changes: the paths are still insert-only, ancestors are still frozen, and
§12a certifies bit-exactness on this model. What changes is the recommended way to *read* the bank.

## 13. Conditional vs unconditional, and what a composition inherits (N27)

> **This is an ANALYSIS experiment, not a method.** Its factor concepts are trained from 96 real images each — far stronger supervision than the single query the out-of-distribution setting provides. It answers *why* score addition fails on one benchmark and works on another; it is not a proposal for how to compose, and no headline claim rests on it.

Score composition behaved in two opposite ways across this repository and the split looked like a
property of the datasets:

| backbone | conditioning channel | embedding composition | score addition |
|---|---|---|---|
| ColorMNIST | one entangled vector per SLOT | F1 0.605 | **0.000** |
| CelebA | one vector per ATTRIBUTE VALUE | pair 0.903 | 0.934–0.965 |
| CelebA-HQ (pretrained) | none | path union 0.17 | 0.50 |

It is not a property of the datasets. N27 tests it directly by **giving the ColorMNIST backbone
per-factor conditionals it does not have** — an adapter concept trained on every SEEN image sharing a
factor value (`fg=f` over all digits and backgrounds, and so on) — and re-running the identical
operator on the identical backbone. Everything stays in-distribution: each factor VALUE appears in
the seen slots, only the COMBINATION is held out.

### 13a. The factor conditionals are real

Each adapter isolates its own factor almost perfectly:

| concept, sampled alone | its own factor | the other two |
|---|---|---|
| `fg0` | fg **1.00** | bg 0.06, digit 0.06 |
| `fg3` | fg **1.00** | bg 0.00, digit 0.09 |
| `bg2` | bg **1.00** | fg 0.00, digit 0.06 |
| `bg3` | bg **0.94** | fg 0.00, digit 0.06 |
| `digit6` | digit **0.94** | fg 0.12, bg 0.44 |
| `digit1` | digit **0.81** | fg 0.31, bg 0.38 |

### 13b. And the operator that produced *nothing* now produces something

> **3-seed numbers.** The single-seed table below reported factor-score at joint 0.167. Over three
> seeds it is **0.042 ± 0.021** against slot-score's **0.000 ± 0.000** — a much smaller gap on joint
> accuracy than one seed suggested. The separation is far clearer on the coverage-aware metric:
> **F1 0.490 ± 0.071 against 0.016 ± 0.016**, and recall 0.697 against 0.034. The qualitative claim —
> the operator works given factor conditionals and produces nothing given slot conditionals — holds;
> the size of the effect on joint accuracy does not. `ours` is 0.562 ± 0.072 / F1 0.679 ± 0.068.

3 held-out compositions, 16 samples:

| arm | joint | digit | fg | bg | F1 | Vendi |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| *real* | *0.792* | *0.792* | *1.00* | *1.00* | *0.956* | *1.67* |
| **ours** (embedding space) | **0.500** | 0.646 | 0.92 | 0.85 | **0.596** | 1.81 |
| **factor-score** (score addition over FACTOR conditionals) | **0.167** | 0.458 | 0.65 | 0.75 | 0.434 | 1.97 |
| slot-score (score addition over SLOT conditionals) | 0.000 | 0.042 | 0.48 | 0.23 | 0.000 | 2.02 |
| factor-union (same adapters, paths activated together) | 0.000 | 0.104 | 0.12 | 0.15 | 0.000 | 2.73 |

**The operator was never the problem.** Given conditionals at the right granularity, score addition
goes from 0.000 to 0.167 joint accuracy and from F1 0.000 to 0.434 — on the same backbone, the same
data and the same arithmetic. ColorMNIST's 120 conditionals are per-slot, and not one of them means
"cyan foreground", so adding them cannot isolate a factor none of them represents.

**But granularity does not close the gap.** Our embedding composition still wins, 0.500 against
0.167, and the reason is visible in the table above: a conditional trained on "every image with
fg = f" is `p(x | fg = f)`, which carries **the marginal of the other factors along with it**. `fg3`
alone puts bg at 0.00 — it does not merely fail to specify the background, it actively imposes the
background distribution of its own training set. Adding three such conditionals makes each one fight
the other two over factors it was never meant to speak about, and the product only partially resolves
the disagreement.

That is the difference from CelebA, where score addition wins. CelebA's factored head is trained with
conditioning built as a **sum of per-attribute embeddings**, so the model has seen those vectors used
compositionally and `attr[k, v]` genuinely means one attribute and stays silent about the rest.
ColorMNIST's factor adapters are fitted post-hoc to marginals and cannot be silent.

### 13c. The rule this gives

> **A composition inherits the granularity — and the marginals — of the conditionals it composes.**
>
> - **No conditioning channel** (unconditional pretrained model): concepts can only be deltas. Deltas
>   must be composed in **score** space; summing them in parameter space interferes (§12e, and
>   `factor-union` at 0.000 here).
> - **Entangled conditionals** (one per combination): score addition cannot reach a novel
>   arrangement, because no conditional isolates a factor. Composition has to happen in a space where
>   the factor structure is explicit — which is what the ideal-words basis provides (R² 0.826), and
>   why our embedding composition is the operator that works here.
> - **Factored conditionals trained compositionally** (CelebA): score addition is the strongest
>   operator available, and better than ours (§11c).
>
> The practical corollary for a bank: **what you put in it determines what you can compose.** A bank
> of slot concepts supports retrieval; a bank of factor concepts supports composition. That is a
> design decision the routing rule already makes — an *arrangement* costs a basis direction and a
> new *part* costs an adapter — and N27 is the measurement showing why the distinction matters
> beyond cost.

**A caveat this experiment carries.** Its factor concepts are trained from 96 real images each, which
is a stronger supervision than the single query the OOD setting provides. It is a probe of the
operator, not a method: it answers "why does score addition fail here", not "here is a better way to
compose". `ours` remains the operator for ColorMNIST, and it wins by 3×.

## 14. Update rules: "disturb the field least" is the wrong objective (N28)

> **Corrected at 3 seeds.** The single-seed table below reported a clean ordering — lowest drift
> composes worst, highest composes best. **The composition half of that does not replicate.** Over
> three seeds the composed-joint differences are inside the noise (high-t 0.188 ± 0.130, uniform
> 0.167 ± 0.042, rehearse 0.146 ± 0.116), so no rule can be declared the best composer from this
> experiment.
>
> | rule | drift | fg alone | bg alone | digit alone | composed joint | F1 |
> |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
> | uniform | 0.040 ± 0.004 | 1.000 | 0.958 | 0.604 ± 0.021 | 0.167 ± 0.042 | 0.434 |
> | **high-t** | 0.043 ± 0.001 | **1.000 ± 0.000** | **1.000 ± 0.000** | **0.729 ± 0.083** | 0.188 ± 0.130 | 0.421 |
> | need-weighted | 0.038 ± 0.002 | 0.958 | 0.792 | 0.625 | 0.083 ± 0.055 | 0.269 |
> | residual | 0.040 ± 0.003 | 1.000 | 0.979 | 0.604 | 0.125 ± 0.125 | 0.319 |
> | **rehearse** | **0.010 ± 0.000** | 0.875 | 0.792 | 0.562 ± 0.108 | 0.146 ± 0.116 | 0.366 |
>
> **What does survive replication**, with separated error bars:
>
> 1. **`rehearse` cuts drift by 4×** (0.010 ± 0.000 against 0.038–0.043) — robust, and it is the only
>    rule that moves drift at all.
> 2. **It pays for that in the concept itself**: fg 0.875 against 1.000, bg 0.792 against 0.958–1.000.
>    Suppressing what an adapter changes does suppress what it *should* change.
> 3. **`high-t` learns the factors best**: bg **1.000 ± 0.000** and digit **0.729 ± 0.083** against
>    uniform's 0.958 and 0.604 ± 0.021.
>
> So the negative result stands — minimizing disturbance is not the objective, since the rule with 4×
> less drift is not better at composing and is measurably worse at learning the factor. But the
> positive claim must be narrowed: **high-noise training learns factors better**, which is a
> per-concept result, not the composition result the single seed suggested. Its composition case
> rests on the pretrained backbone (§12c), where it was the difference between working and not.

The insert-only guarantee covers an INACTIVE path. Composition evaluates an adapter on inputs it
never saw, and nothing certifies its behaviour there — so the natural hypothesis is that an update
which changes the frozen field only where it must should compose better than one that changes it
everywhere. N28 measures that directly, on identical data, budget and architecture.

`drift` is how far the model moves on conditionings the concept is *not* for, with its path **active**:
`||eps_path(x,u,c_seen) − eps_frozen(x,u,c_seen)||` over trained classes.

| rule | drift | fg alone | bg alone | digit alone | composed joint | recall | F1 | wall |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| uniform | 0.0440 | 1.00 | 1.00 | 0.81 | 0.062 | 0.461 | 0.146 | 52s |
| **high-t** (t ≥ 0.5) | **0.0469** *(worst)* | 1.00 | 1.00 | **0.88** | **0.104** | **0.882** | **0.181** | 52s |
| need-weighted | 0.0406 | 0.98 | 0.94 | 0.81 | 0.042 | 0.684 | 0.140 | 52s |
| residual | 0.0450 | 1.00 | 0.98 | 0.79 | 0.062 | 0.875 | 0.110 | 69s |
| **rehearse** | **0.0106** *(best)* | 0.92 | 0.90 | 0.62 | **0.000** *(worst)* | 0.781 | 0.105 | 111s |

**The hypothesis is false, and cleanly so.** The rule with the *lowest* drift composes *worst*, and
the rule with the *highest* drift composes *best*. `rehearse` suppresses drift by a factor of four —
0.0106 against 0.041–0.047 — and reaches joint 0.000, because a penalty for changing anything also
prevents the adapter from changing what it should: its factor accuracies are the lowest in the table
(digit 0.62 against 0.81–0.88).

So "an update that reflects the existing score field" does **not** mean "an update that disturbs it
least". Drift is not a cost to be minimized; it is the mechanism by which a conditional differs from
the unconditional model at all.

**What does work is choosing WHERE to spend the update.** `high-t` — train only at noise levels above
the midpoint — wins on composed joint accuracy (0.104), on recall (0.882 against uniform's 0.461) and
on F1, at identical wall clock. This is the pretrained-backbone finding (§12b) reproducing on a
backbone we trained ourselves and on a completely different dataset, which is what makes it a
property of diffusion adaptation rather than of that one model's data mismatch:

> A factor's *identity* is decided at high noise. Capacity spent at low noise goes to texture the
> model already renders correctly, and buys nothing.

`need-weighted` — sampling noise levels in proportion to the frozen model's own error — is the more
principled version of the same idea and it does *not* beat the blunt threshold (joint 0.042 against
0.104). Its measured "need" is largest at the noisiest levels where every model does badly, so it
over-weights the extreme tail rather than the semantically decisive middle-to-high band. The crude
rule is better, and that is worth stating rather than presenting the elegant one.

**Recommendation adopted:** train concept adapters at high noise levels. It costs nothing, it is one
line, and it improves composition and coverage on both backbones tested.

## 15. Batch addition on a pretrained backbone (N26), and where its speedup actually comes from

"Do not learn one instance at a time into the manifold" is a requirement, not a preference: a stream
that arrives in groups must be absorbed in groups or the cost of the manifold becomes the cost of its
history. N4 established batch addition on our own 6.8M UNet — **1.7× faster** than K sequential
consolidations at identical accuracy, retention still exactly 1.000, and genuinely shared nodes (6
against 3). N26 asks the same of `google/ddpm-celebahq-256`, and needs its own trainer, because
`consolidate_batch` samples our cosine schedule and a pretrained DDPM has its own.

4 concepts (`Eyeglasses`, `Smiling`, `Wearing_Hat`, `Male`), 96 real images each, 40 epochs:

| arm | wall | attribute rate | Vendi | nodes | shared | **bit-identical** | params |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| sequential (4 optimizations) | 777s | **0.75** | 1.46 | 5 | 3 | **1.00** | 0.98M |
| batch (1 optimization) | 777s | 0.58 | 1.42 | 5 | 3 | **1.00** | 0.98M |
| batch-shared (1 optimization, group node) | 783s | 0.67 | 1.45 | 6 | **6** | **1.00** | 1.18M |

**The requirement is met: K concepts go in as one update, and every concept is still bit-identical
afterwards on a 113.7M-parameter model we did not train.** `batch-shared` additionally creates twice
the sharing (6 shared node-uses against 3), which is the thing a sequential run structurally cannot
do — a shared ancestor accumulates gradient from all K members inside one optimization, where
sequential can only hand a later concept a frozen warm start.

**But there is no wall-clock speedup here (1.00×), and that is the more useful finding.** Comparing
against N4's 1.7× locates where batching actually pays:

> Batch addition amortizes the **generative pool sampling**, not the optimizer. On ColorMNIST each
> concept needs a pool sampled from the model — one reverse-diffusion run instead of K is the whole
> saving. Here the concepts are trained on **real images**, so there is no pool, and interleaving K
> concepts' minibatches costs exactly the same number of optimizer steps as running them in sequence.

So the efficiency claim should be stated conditionally rather than generally: batching is a large win
when concepts are distilled from *generated* pools, and a wash when they come from *real* data. What
it buys unconditionally is the shared structure and the preservation guarantee, both of which hold on
both backbones.

Attribute rates (0.75 / 0.58 / 0.67) are over 4 samples × 4 concepts and the differences are within
what that resolution can distinguish; the honest reading is that batching does not *improve* concept
quality, and the case for it rests on cost and sharing.

## 16. How far does batch addition scale, and what does it cost? (N4, K-sweep)

N4 compared batch against sequential at one batch size. This sweeps K with the total held fixed at
**12 concepts**, so `sequential` is the same run throughout (a useful control: it lands at 680–690s
and ACC 0.547 every time) and only the size of each update changes.

| K (concepts per update) | wall | speedup | ACC | FGT | **bit-identical** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| sequential (K=1, one at a time) | 686s | — | **0.547** | 0.000 | **1.00** |
| 1 | 561s | 1.23× | 0.370 | 0.000 | **1.00** |
| 2 | 429s | 1.59× | 0.375 | 0.000 | **1.00** |
| 4 | 341s | 2.00× | 0.453 | 0.000 | **1.00** |
| 8 | 297s | 2.29× | 0.396 | 0.000 | **1.00** |
| **12** (all of them in ONE update) | **260s** | **2.64×** | 0.422 | 0.000 | **1.00** |

**Two things scale cleanly.** The speedup grows monotonically with K and does not plateau by K=12 —
all twelve concepts absorbed in a single update run 2.64× faster than twelve updates. And
`retention_exact` is **1.00 at every K**, with FGT 0.000: batch size is a free efficiency knob and
not a safety trade, which is the property the insert-only design was supposed to give and here it is
measured across a 12× range.

**One thing costs.** Batch accuracy sits at 0.37–0.45 against sequential's 0.547 — a real gap, and
one an earlier reading of N4 (8 concepts, batch 4) missed because at that size the two matched at
0.609. The sweep localizes it precisely:

> **The loss is NOT in the batched optimizer.** At **K = 1** the batch path already scores 0.370
> against sequential's 0.547 — and at K = 1 the optimizer is doing identical work in both. Whatever
> costs the accuracy is upstream of consolidation.

Upstream, `learn_concepts_batch` differed from `learn_concept` in **two** ways:

1. **discovery configuration** — the batch path is handed an explicit `disc`
   (`n_starts=16, iters=120`); the sequential path called `compose(xq)` and got
   `candidates_ascent`'s defaults. The two modes were not running the same discovery;
2. **pooled sampling** — the batch path draws all K pools from one reverse-diffusion run.

### 16b. The gap was the confound, and batching costs nothing

Re-running with the sequential path given the batch path's discovery configuration
(`learn_concept_disc`), at K = 1 where batching can do nothing:

| matched discovery | wall | ACC | FGT | bit-identical |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **K = 1** batch | 521s | 0.370 | 0.000 | 1.00 |
| **K = 1** sequential | 521s | 0.370 | 0.000 | 1.00 |
| **K = 12** batch (all in ONE update) | **249s** | **0.422** | 0.000 | 1.00 |
| **K = 12** sequential | 532s | 0.370 | 0.000 | 1.00 |

**At K = 1 the two are identical on every column** — the control works, and the entire 0.547 → 0.370
gap was the discovery setting rather than batching. The old sequential runs were quietly using a
*more expensive and better* discovery configuration (686s against 521s) than the batch runs they were
compared with. Pooled sampling is exonerated, the batched optimizer is exonerated, and the earlier N4
conclusion that the two modes reach the same accuracy was right; the K-sweep's apparent gap was an
artifact of this experiment.

**And at K = 12 batching is FREE: 2.15x faster (3 seeds) at no cost to accuracy (+0.035 +/- 0.037, not distinguishable from zero)**
(0.422 against 0.370), at FGT 0.000 and bit-identical 1.00. The accuracy gain is the shared-ancestor
effect, and it is the one thing a sequential run cannot reproduce at any budget: a group node trained
by all twelve members inside one optimization, where sequential can only hand a later concept a
frozen warm start. Absorbing the group that arrived is not merely cheaper than absorbing it one at a
time — on this benchmark it is also better.

Two lessons worth keeping separate from the result. The first is the finding: **batch addition is
free.** Same accuracy, same forgetting, same bit-exact preservation, and monotonically faster as K
grows. The second is methodological, and it is the third time this project has been bitten the same
way: a comparison between two code paths silently compared two *configurations*, and the difference
was read as a property of the mechanism. The fix was to make the sequential path's discovery explicit
rather than defaulted.

It also explains §15's result rather than conflicting with it: on the pretrained backbone there are
no pools at all (concepts train on real images), so there is neither the 2.64× saving **nor** the
accuracy cost, and batch and sequential come out at 777s with retention 1.00 both ways.

## 17. Synthesis: are these three ideas possible in this framework?

Three questions were put to the framework. All three are answerable in it, two came back differently
from the way they were posed, and one produced a design rule.

### Q1 — conditional vs unconditional, and what compositions inherit

**Answerable, and the answer is a rule.** The framework already spans the whole range: a
per-slot-conditional backbone (ColorMNIST), a compositionally-conditioned one (CelebA), and a fully
unconditional pretrained one (CelebA-HQ). N27 turns the coincidence into a controlled test by giving
the ColorMNIST backbone per-factor conditionals it lacks and re-running the same operator.

> **A composition inherits the granularity — and the marginals — of the conditionals it composes.**
>
> | the model has | concepts are | compose them in | evidence |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | no conditioning channel | deltas | **score** space; parameter space interferes | §12e: 0.50 vs 0.17 |
> | entangled per-combination conditionals | vectors in a factorizable space | **embedding** space | §13b: 0.500 vs 0.167 |
> | factored, compositionally-trained conditionals | per-attribute conditionals | **score** space | §11c: 0.965 vs 0.903 |
>
> Score addition on ColorMNIST went 0.000 → 0.167 purely by changing the *granularity* of what was
> composed, on the same backbone and the same arithmetic — so the operator was never the problem. It
> does not close the gap to embedding composition (0.500) because a conditional fitted to "images
> with fg = f" is `p(x | fg = f)` and drags the other factors' marginals with it: `fg3` alone forces
> bg to 0.00 rather than staying silent.

The practical corollary is a design rule for the bank: **what you put in it determines what you can
compose.** Slot concepts support retrieval; factor concepts support composition. And on an
unconditional backbone the bank *is* the conditioning channel — it turns an unconditional model into
a conditional one whose conditionals are exactly its members.

### Q2 — updates that reflect the existing learned score field

**Answerable, and the intuition is false as stated.** N28 measures it directly: `drift` is how far the
model moves on conditionings a concept is *not* for, with its path **active** — the case the
insert-only guarantee does not cover and the case composition actually exercises.

The lowest-drift rule composes **worst** (rehearse: drift 0.0106, composed joint 0.000) and the
highest-drift rule composes **best** (high-t: drift 0.0469, composed joint 0.104, recall 0.882
against uniform's 0.461). Drift is not a cost to be minimized — it is the mechanism by which a
conditional differs from the unconditional model at all, and an objective that suppresses it
suppresses the concept too.

What *does* work is choosing **where** to spend the update:

> **Train concept adapters at high noise levels.** A factor's identity is settled there; capacity
> spent at low noise goes to texture the model already renders correctly. Free, one line, and it
> improves composition and coverage on **both** backbones and **both** datasets tested (§12c, §14) —
> which is what makes it a property of diffusion adaptation rather than of one model's data.

The more principled version — sampling noise levels in proportion to the frozen model's own error —
*loses* to the blunt threshold (0.042 against 0.104), because measured "need" peaks in the noisiest
tail where every model does badly rather than in the semantically decisive band. Reported that way
round rather than the flattering way.

### Q3 — batch updates, not one instance at a time

**Already possible, now verified at scale and on a model we did not train, and it is free.**

| | evidence |
|---|---|
| K concepts in ONE update | `consolidate_batch` / `learn_concepts_batch`; N26 for the pretrained path |
| preservation survives it | bit-identical **1.00 at every K from 1 to 12**, and 1.00 on all three pretrained arms |
| it scales | **2.64× faster at K = 12**, monotonic in K, no plateau |
| it costs nothing in quality | K = 1 matched-discovery control: **identical**; at K = 12 batch is **better** (0.422 vs 0.370) (§16b) |
| it creates structure sequential cannot | shared ancestors trained by all K members at once (6 shared node-uses against 3) |

With one qualification worth stating rather than burying: **the speedup comes from amortizing the
generative pool sampling, not the optimizer.** On the pretrained backbone, where concepts train on
real images and there is no pool, batch and sequential both take 777s — batching still preserves
exactness and still builds shared structure, but it buys no time. So "batch addition is fast" is
conditional on distilling from generated pools; "batch addition is safe and structural" is not
conditional on anything.

### What this changed in the method

- `sample_composed` — score-space composition over bank members is now a first-class operation, since
  three independent measurements say path union is the wrong way to read the bank (§12e, §13b, §14).
- `learn_concept_disc` — the sequential path's discovery configuration is explicit, so mode
  comparisons isolate the mode (§16b).
- High-noise-only adaptation is the recommended update rule for concept adapters.
- `inject(..., exclude=)` — on a pretrained backbone, keep adapters off the time-conditioning path.

## 18. Held-out severity: does this collapse as the combination grid thins? (N1 `--extra-holdout`)

MOSAIC (ICML 2026) argues that benchmarks like ColorMNIST are the *easy* regime — single-object,
densely covered — and that compositional generalization collapses as more combinations are withheld.
Inheriting the benchmark means inheriting the criticism, so the grid is thinned and the whole
showdown re-run on each thinned backbone. `--extra-holdout E` withholds E further slots on top of the
benchmark's 40, choosing only slots whose every attribute **value** still appears elsewhere — so
combinations are removed and primitives never are.

Three backbones trained from scratch, one seed each:

| extra withheld | seen slots | **ours** joint / F1 | `poe-guided` joint | `inversion` joint | `composable-diff` joint |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (the benchmark) | 120 | 0.492 / 0.520 | **0.892** | 0.425 | 0.000 |
| 20 | 100 | **0.683 / 0.750** | **0.942** | 0.450 | 0.025 |
| 40 (grid half empty) | 80 | 0.467 / **0.372** | **0.292** | 0.392 | 0.008 |

**The headline: at the most severe setting the companion method collapses and ours does not.**
`poe-guided` runs 0.892 → 0.942 → **0.292**, losing two thirds of its accuracy once 80 of 160 slots
are withheld. Ours runs 0.492 → 0.683 → 0.467 — no trend, and certainly no collapse. `inversion` is
similarly stable (0.425 → 0.450 → 0.392) and `composable-diff` stays at ~0 throughout, as everywhere
else on this benchmark.

The mechanism is visible in the design rather than only in the numbers: `poe-guided` composes
prototypes *discovered in the backbone's own score field*, so thinning that field thins what it can
discover. Our concepts are consolidated from the query and then **kept**, so the backbone's coverage
sets the starting point rather than the ceiling.

**Two caveats, both real.**

1. **One seed per severity level, and each level is a different backbone.** Backbone-to-backbone
   variance is therefore not separated from the severity effect, and ours being *better* at E=20
   than at E=0 is the clearest sign of that — it is not plausible that withholding 20 more
   combinations helps. The E=40 collapse of `poe-guided` is large enough (a factor of three) to
   survive that noise; the finer ordering is not.
2. **`--extra-holdout` never removes a primitive**, by construction. So this sweeps *combination*
   sparsity, which is what MOSAIC's criticism is about, and says nothing about withholding attribute
   values — measured separately in §7c (n21) and §5q2 (n11), where a genuinely new value needs a
   weight update from real images and cannot be composed at all.

### 11e. Every held-out composition, scored — and how the case study is chosen (N29)

The case-study figure had been showing three compositions picked at random by `ood_queries(seed=0)`.
Two of the three landed on the same colour pair and one of those was a poor case, which makes the
figure simultaneously unrepresentative and monotonous. Choosing nicer slots by hand would fix the
look and destroy the evidence, so the selection was made a measurement: **score all 40 held-out
compositions, report the distribution, and pick from it by a stated rule.**

**The full distribution** (ours, calibrated guidance, 16 samples each):

> joint accuracy **mean 0.441, median 0.438, range [0.00, 0.94]** · F1 mean 0.582, median 0.644

**And it is strongly colour-dependent**, which the old three-case figure could not have shown:

| colour pair | n | median joint | best | worst |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| green on navy | 10 | **0.719** | 0.94 | 0.25 |
| pink on dark_purple | 10 | **0.688** | 0.88 | 0.31 |
| cyan on deep_red | 10 | 0.375 | 0.62 | 0.00 |
| yellow on dark_brown | 10 | **0.125** | 0.44 | 0.00 |

A **5.8× spread in median accuracy** across the four held-out colour pairs. `yellow on dark_brown` is
the hard case — a low-contrast warm foreground on a warm background — and `green on navy` is the easy
one. Any figure showing three random slots is as likely to describe one end as the other, and the
mean over 40 (0.441) is close to the 3-seed n9 number (0.539 over 5 cases), so neither is
misrepresenting the method; the *figure* was.

**The selection rule.** ColorMNIST's held-out tier is exactly 4 colour pairs × 10 digits, so the rule
that spans the benchmark is **one case per colour pair, at that pair's median**:

| slot | composition | joint |
|---|---|---|
| 152 | digit 9, cyan on deep_red | 0.38 |
| 69 | digit 4, green on navy | 0.75 |
| 126 | digit 7, pink on dark_purple | 0.69 |
| 19 | digit 1, yellow on dark_brown | 0.19 |

Four different digits, four different colour pairs, each the median of its pair — so the figure is
what a reader should expect to reproduce rather than the best of forty. `--select best-per-pair` and
`worst-per-pair` render the tails, and the figure always states which rule produced it.

## 19. The visual artifacts: a refuted hypothesis, and where they actually come from

Our samples carry a scattered high-frequency texture that **no metric in this repository could see**.
Composition accuracy, precision/recall, density/coverage and Vendi are all indifferent to it: a
speckled digit is still the right digit in the right colours. `evals.speckle()` measures it directly
— total variation relative to real images of the same class, plus a literal count of pixels differing
from all four neighbours.

**Only the first of those two numbers turned out to be usable, and saying so changes the diagnosis.**
The isolated-pixel count sits at the noise floor for 32×32 ColorMNIST: across every stage measured
below it ranges 0.00000–0.00010 of pixels, which on a batch of 16 images is **0 to 1.6 pixels in the
entire batch**, against 0.98 for real data. Worse, as a *ratio* it divided by a denominator that is
nearly zero on real images, and the interval sweep duly reported `isolated_ratio` of **20345 on two
rows and ~0.2 on three others for image sets that are statistically identical** (`slurm/3673660.out`).
The denominator is now floored at one pixel in ten thousand and `tests/test_speckle.py` asserts the
ratio stays bounded, that the detector still fires on unambiguous dots, and that a set scored against
itself is exactly 1.0. No number published in this document ever came from the broken column.

The substantive consequence: **the artifact is not isolated dots at all.** The stage with the highest
total variation (the frozen backbone on a trained class, 1.593) has *zero* isolated pixels. What we
had been calling "scattered pixels" is broadband roughness — elevated TV across the whole image — and
that is what the rest of this section measures.

### 19a. The obvious hypothesis, refuted

Guidance applied at **low** noise levels amplifies high-frequency error, and this repository had
already demonstrated exactly that for the analytic teacher: running the companion method on our
annealed schedule rather than its own Eq. 14 injected speckle into the case-study figure (§5l-bis).
We were applying a **constant** classifier-free weight at every noise level when sampling the
distillation pool, which is the same mistake in our own half of the pipeline. The standard fix is to
restrict guidance to an interval (Kynkäänniemi et al., NeurIPS 2024, arXiv:2404.07724), so that was
implemented and swept.

| guidance interval | tv vs real | joint | recall |
|---|---|---|---|
| none (constant, the old behaviour) | 1.279 | 0.479 | 0.788 |
| [0.1, 1.0] | 1.272 | 0.458 | 0.791 |
| [0.2, 1.0] | 1.261 | 0.417 | 0.795 |
| [0.3, 1.0] | 1.271 | 0.479 | 0.785 |
| [0.5, 1.0] | 1.275 | 0.500 | 0.778 |

**No effect** — the five rows of `figures/extra/diag_guidance_interval.png` are indistinguishable. And in
hindsight it could not have had one: `sample_concept` already samples at **guidance 1.0**, so there
is no CFG at sampling time to restrict. The interval only touched pool generation, and the adapter
does not visibly inherit texture from the pool.

The knob is kept (`sample_embedding(..., interval=)`) because it is correct and cheap, and the
negative result is kept because it is the evidence that the artifact is *not* CFG.

### 19b. Where the texture actually comes from

Walking the pipeline stage by stage, each against real images of the same composition
(`tests/diag_artifact_source.py`):

| stage | tv vs real |
|---|---|
| *real images* | *0.993* |
| **the frozen backbone, on a class it was TRAINED on** | **1.593** |
| the backbone at the composed vector, 50 steps | 1.207 |
| the backbone at the composed vector, 200 steps | 1.116 |
| our adapter, 50 steps | 1.278 |
| our adapter, 200 steps | 1.183 |

**The texture is the backbone's, and our pipeline is smoother than the backbone is on its own trained
classes.** The 6.8M-parameter UNet at 32×32 renders a class it was explicitly trained on at 1.59×
real total variation — worse than anything downstream of it. No change to the composer, the pool, the
adapter or the sampler can remove an artifact that the frozen model puts there.

Two things do help, and only one of them is free:

1. **More sampling steps.** Monotonic in both stages: 1.278 → 1.214 → 1.183 for the adapter across
   50 / 100 / 200 steps, at no cost to accuracy. The case-study figure now renders at 200 steps and
   says so; the tables stay at 50 so every arm is compared at one budget.
2. **A better backbone.** This is the real fix and it is the same lever as §12 — the 113.7M-parameter
   pretrained model produces clean 256×256 faces, and the artifacts in that figure came from *our*
   adaptation of it rather than from the model.

The general lesson is the one this project keeps relearning: **a defect nothing measures will survive
every experiment.** Three of the corrections in this repository were found by looking at pictures,
and this is the fourth.

## 20. Distance from the prototype is a dial, not a property of the method (N30)

Every composition table in this document reports a single operating point, and that has been quietly
unfair to both methods. A composition method produces two things a user might want independently:

* a **prototype** — what the combination looks like, canonically;
* **exemplars** — instances of it that differ from one another.

This repository has been reporting the far end of that axis and the companion paper the near end,
which is exactly why their samples score high joint accuracy at recall 0.001 while ours score lower
accuracy with real coverage (§11d). Neither is a property of the method. Both are a *choice*.

**The dial.** Start the reverse process from a partially noised prototype instead of from pure noise,
and integrate from that noise level:

```
x_init = sqrt(abar(s)) * prototype + sqrt(1 - abar(s)) * noise,    integrate from u = s
```

`s → 0` leaves the prototype nearly intact (near-copies); `s → 1` destroys it (unconstrained draws
from the method's own conditional). The same axis applies to both methods, which is what makes them
comparable on it. `score.sample()` already implemented the noising, so this is a sweep, not a new
mechanism.

`experiments/n30_prototype_distance.py`, 3 seeds × 4 compositions × 16 samples, mean ± s.e.m.:

| s | ours d(proto) | ours recall | ours F1 | ours Vendi | poe d(proto) | poe recall | poe F1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.60 | 0.071±0.003 | 0.003±0.002 | 0.007±0.003 | 1.146 | 0.058±0.004 | 0.001±0.001 | 0.002 |
| 0.70 | 0.087±0.003 | 0.062±0.007 | 0.112±0.013 | 1.253 | 0.058±0.004 | 0.000 | 0.000 |
| 0.80 | 0.109±0.004 | 0.291±0.055 | 0.410±0.058 | 1.436 | 0.058±0.004 | 0.000 | 0.000 |
| 0.85 | 0.123±0.004 | 0.541±0.051 | 0.620±0.030 | 1.567 | 0.057±0.004 | 0.000 | 0.000 |
| **0.90** | **0.136±0.003** | **0.688±0.027** | **0.669±0.018** | **1.692** | 0.056±0.004 | 0.000 | 0.000 |
| 0.95 | 0.149±0.003 | 0.790±0.015 | 0.638±0.008 | 1.790 | 0.055±0.004 | 0.000 | 0.000 |
| 1.00 | 0.165±0.003 | 0.843±0.018 | 0.599±0.015 | 1.884 | 0.055±0.004 | 0.000 | 0.000 |

![the dial](../figures/case_prototype_distance.png)

### 20a. The dial is ours alone

**For our method every quantity moves together and monotonically**: distance to the prototype spans
0.071 → 0.165 (**2.32×**), Vendi 1.15 → 1.88, recall 0.003 → 0.843, while joint accuracy trades the
other way, 0.953 → 0.490. That is the shape a controllable knob is supposed to have, and it means the
two ends of the axis are both reachable from one consolidated concept — no retraining, one argument.

**For the companion method the dial does nothing.** Distance to the prototype moves 0.058 → 0.055
(0.94×, i.e. not at all), Vendi stays at 1.07, and recall is 0.000 at every setting. The striking row
is `s = 1.0`: the prototype has been destroyed and the process begins from essentially pure noise, and
the samples *still* land 0.055 from the prototype. Guidance toward the analytic teacher re-collapses
the trajectory onto the mode regardless of where it started — which is the same collapse that gives
poe-guided joint 0.839 at F1 0.003 in §11d, now shown to be structural rather than a tuning choice.

One asymmetry, stated because it cuts against us and does not change the conclusion: our arm's
`x_init` is the prototype mean exactly, while the teacher arm's is a draw from the teacher's own
Gaussian, `mu + sqrt(var) * z`, and is therefore *already diverse* before any noise is added. The
companion method is thus given the more favourable starting condition and still collapses.

### 20b. The operating point, corrected

A coarse first sweep (`--strengths 0.2 … 1.0`, one seed, 2 compositions) read the elbow at **s = 0.8**
and that was provisionally adopted as the default. **The 3-seed fine sweep does not support it.**
F1 is *not* monotone in `s`: it peaks at **s = 0.90** (0.669 ± 0.018), and s = 0.8 sits at
0.410 ± 0.058 — more than four standard errors below, not a rounding difference. The settings
statistically indistinguishable from the best (within 2 s.e.) are **{0.85, 0.90, 0.95}**.

The recommended setting is therefore **s = 0.90**, and s = 0.8 is recorded here as superseded rather
than deleted.

> **Overridden for the figure on 2026-08-13 — see §20c.** `make_case_studies.DEFAULT_DIAL` was set to
> **0.8** on a judgement about colour faithfulness in the rendered panels, i.e. deliberately against
> this sweep, which puts 0.8 more than **four standard errors** below 0.90 (F1 0.410 ± 0.058 against
> 0.669 ± 0.018, 3 seeds). The metric's preference is unchanged and stated here; the override is a
> presentation choice with its cost recorded in §20c. Its scope is exactly one constant read by one
> script: `sample_concept` still defaults to `u_start = 1.0`, so **no table in this document moves**,
> and no comparison is run at a tuned operating point. The coarse sweep was not wrong about the *shape* — F1 does rise steeply through 0.8 —
it simply had no sample points between 0.8 and 1.0 and so could not see that the rise continues.

**This is a default for exemplar generation, not a global change, and no table in this document moves
because of it.** `sample_concept` still defaults to `u_start = 1.0` (start from pure noise, ignore the
prototype), which is what every arm in every comparison table was and remains run at — changing that
default would silently re-scale results that were collected under it, and would compare our arm at a
tuned operating point against baselines at an untuned one. `s = 0.90` is the setting to pass when the
thing being asked for is *exemplars of a concept*, which is the case-study figures.

This is the second time in this repository that a conclusion drawn from a coarse single-seed sweep
did not survive replication (§14 was the first). Both are kept in the record.

**Confirmed a third time, on the case-study renders themselves**, once those were run at the paper's
discovery budget and at the calibrated pool guidance (`results/case_colormnist*.json`, 4 held-out
compositions selected by the median-per-pair rule):

| dial `s` | joint accuracy | recall (coverage) |
|---|---|---|
| 0.80 | 0.667 | 0.495 |
| **0.90** | **0.708** | 0.881 |
| 1.00 | 0.542 | **0.907** |

**0.90 is the only setting that is near-best on both axes.** 0.8 buys accuracy by halving coverage;
1.0 keeps coverage and loses accuracy. `make_case_studies.py` therefore defaults to `--dial 0.9`, and
the canonical figure is the one rendered at it. The tables are untouched, for the reason given above.

*Which placement rule these were run under.* The sweep above predates `cu` becoming the default, so
all three rows are `ll`. Re-rendering the selected setting under `cu` moves `ours` from 0.708 to
**0.667** on the same four compositions — one case of the four drops, the other three are identical —
which is the same small, mixed effect §24 reports for the rule change generally. The *selection* is
unaffected: nothing in the ordering depends on the rule, and 0.9 remains the only setting near-best
on both axes. The canonical figure is the `cu` render, matching the current defaults.

Read the baseline columns alongside: `poe-guided` scores **0.833 joint at 0.000 recall** and
`query-only` **0.875 at 0.000**. They are more accurate and produce *no coverage at all* — the same
correct image repeatedly. That is the trade this project exists to name, and it is why accuracy alone
cannot separate composing from copying.

**What this does not claim.** The dial does not create coverage that the concept does not have: at
`s = 1.0` we are simply sampling the concept's conditional, which is what every other table in this
document already reports. What is new is that the *near* end is reachable at all, and that the
companion method cannot reach it or leave it.

## 21. The metric policy: why KID per composition and FID only pooled

This repository reports FID where Inception is in domain, and it took a measurement to decide *how*.

`evals.frechet_distance` computes the Fréchet statistic on low-pass **pixel** features and is
deliberately not called FID: Inception-V3 was trained on natural images at 299×299, and 32×32
recoloured MNIST is outside its domain, so a number computed there would look comparable to the
literature without being comparable to anything. That stands. `lifelong_ood/fid.py` adds the standard
number for the rest of the suite — CelebA, CIFAR-10, CLEVR — using `pytorch-fid`'s **TF-ported**
Inception weights rather than torchvision's, because every published value it would be compared
against uses the ported ones.

### 21a. FID is unusable at the sample counts this benchmark runs at

Scoring **two halves of one distribution** — true distance exactly zero, so every number reported is
pure bias (`tests/test_fid.py`):

| n | FID | KID |
|---|---|---|
| 32 | **50.663** | −0.00177 |
| 64 | 35.092 | 0.00091 |
| 128 | 24.438 | 0.00102 |
| 256 | 16.504 | 0.00238 |
| 512 | 10.743 | 0.00164 |
| 1024 | 5.603 | 0.00036 |

**FID reads 50.7 between identical distributions at n = 32** — larger than many real FID differences
reported in the literature — and it decays monotonically with n. It is tracking the sample count, not
the data. KID (Bińkowski et al., ICLR 2018 — the unbiased MMD² estimator) stays within 0.0024 of zero
at every n.

Our per-composition sample counts are 16–100. So the policy is:

* **KID per composition**, which is where every OOD claim in this document lives;
* **FID pooled over ≥ 2048 samples**, for dataset-level quality only — and `fid()` raises rather than
  returning a number below that, because the failure mode being guarded against is a small-n FID
  getting quoted as if it meant something;
* **pixel-feature Fréchet** retained for ColorMNIST and other non-natural data, labelled as not
  comparable to published FID.

A useful corollary for the OOD claim specifically: the withheld combination has *real images* — they
exist in the dataset and were simply never trained on — so KID **against the held-out real images**
directly scores "did you generate the unseen combination," rather than merely measuring prettiness.

### 21b. The layout trap, pinned

This repository's flat vectors are HWC and the model pipeline *reinterprets* them as CHW — harmless
there, because training and sampling apply the same permutation. It is not harmless for Inception,
which must see an upright image or its features mean nothing, and which will return a confident
number either way. `fid.py` reshapes the display way; the test asserts that a CHW-permuted copy of a
set scores **0.344** against the same set's **−1.4e−03**, so a silent regression cannot pass. This is
the third guard against the permutation that cost three hours in §5 (`tests/test_layout.py`).

## 22. Certified deletion (N31): removing a concept without disturbing the others

Every lifelong result here is about adding. Deletion is the same question with the sign flipped, and
deployed systems are asked it constantly — a retracted source, a right-to-be-forgotten request, a
concept that turned out to be wrong. It is also where parameter *isolation* and parameter
*protection* differ most sharply, and nothing here had measured it.

For us, deletion is not a new mechanism: a concept **is** a set of nodes on a path, and the frozen
base is never written to, so removing one is dropping the nodes only it uses
(`bank.remove_concept`). The honest baseline is what a method that folds updates into shared weights
would actually have to do — **retrain from scratch on the remaining concepts**.

10 concepts on ColorMNIST, deleting the middle one (`experiments/n31_deletion.py`):

| arm | survivors bit-identical | max drift | deleted name gone | params freed | seconds |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **ours (structural)** | **9 / 9** | **0.000e+00** | yes | 551,408 | **0.002** |
| retrain-without-it | 0 / 9 | 9.930e−01 | yes | 0 | 258.4 |

The frozen base is bit-identical too, which is the other half of "nothing else moved". Deletion is a
dict operation, so it is O(1) in the stream where retraining is O(K) model fits — reported as the two
absolute times rather than as a ratio, since dividing 258 s by ~0 would be an artifact of the
denominator rather than a measurement.

**Forgetting is total, not a fallback.** The field resolves a concept name *through* `bank.concepts`,
so once the bank drops it the name is genuinely unknown and `eps()` raises. It does not quietly
revert to the base and keep generating something.

### 22a. What deletion does *not* recover

Deletion makes a concept unreachable. It does **not** generally reclaim its storage, and the first
run of this experiment hid that behind a lucky choice of victim. Measured for every concept before
deleting any:

| reclaimable fraction of a concept's path parameters | min | median | max |
|---|---|---|---|
| | **0.000** | 0.250 | 0.333 |

Two separate reasons, and both are structural:

1. **Only the leaf is private.** A path is root → intermediate → leaf, and the ancestors are shared
   with other concepts, so at best ⅓ of the parameters on a path can go.
2. **A leaf can be adopted.** `_descend` may route a later concept *through* an earlier concept's
   leaf, promoting it to an ancestor. Dropping it would break the descendant, so it is kept —
   **3 / 10** concepts here are in that state, and for those only the 256-float embedding is
   reclaimable.

So the accurate claim is *certified forgetting with partial reclamation*, not "deletion frees the
concept". The guard that produces this — the nodes to drop are the path minus the union of every
other concept's path — is what makes the 9/9 above hold; without it, deleting an adopted leaf would
silently corrupt its descendants.

## 23. Order invariance (N32): a prediction this repository made and had to retract

Insert-only preservation invites an appealing conjecture: since nothing shared is ever written to, a
concept's parameters should not depend on what came *after* it, so the same concept set presented in
a different order should give **bit-identical** concepts. That would be a proof-shaped result, much
stronger than the usual "accuracy varies by X across orderings" that continual learning reports.

**It is false, and the experiment says so.** 6 concepts, 4 orderings, against the reference order
(`experiments/n32_order.py`):

| arm | bit-identical | max drift | same path | bit-identical \| same path |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ours | **0.000** | 1.937 | 0.056 | 0.000 |
| unfrozen-ancestors (control) | 0.000 | 1.939 | 0.056 | 0.000 |

Ours is **indistinguishable from the control that does not freeze anything at all**, which is the
cleanest possible refutation.

**Why, and what the real property is.** Placement is computed against whatever tree exists at
insertion time, so a different order gives a concept a different parent — only **5.6%** of concepts
kept their path at all. And even those were not bit-identical, because a shared ancestor's *contents*
depend on which concept created it and what trained it, both of which the order changes.

The property this repository actually has, and which §5/§7 measure, is **forward-only**: once a
concept is consolidated, nothing that arrives later changes it. That is not the same as order
invariance, and conflating the two was an error of reasoning on my part, not a bug in the code. The
narrower structural claim does survive and is true by construction: `insert_batch` computes every
member's placement against **one snapshot** before creating anything, so the structure produced by a
*batch* does not depend on the arrival order *within* that batch (§15).

Kept rather than deleted, alongside §14 and §20b, as the third prediction here that replication
refused.

## 24. What the hierarchy is and is not doing (N34, N36, and the rank sweep)

Three experiments run for different reasons converged on one answer, so they are reported together.
The short version: **the shared node is real, and it does not help.** What is load-bearing is the
sealing discipline, not the tree.

### 24a. Shared nodes are live, and genuinely jointly formed

Two things had to be true before "reuse" could mean anything, and both are.

*Live.* `B` is zero-initialized, so a node can carry a large `A` and emit exactly nothing until `B`
grows — `diag_shared_node` raised that possibility for the unfrozen arm, and if it held for group
nodes then every reuse arm would be identical by construction. Measured
(`tests/diag_ancestor_live.py`, 3 concepts sharing one group node):

| node | owners | ‖B‖ | ‖BA‖ / ‖W‖ |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (root) | 3 | **0.00000** | **0.000e+00** |
| 1 (group) | 3 | 7.291 | **8.907e−02** |
| 2, 3, 4 (leaves) | 1 | ~4.2 | ~3.8e−02 |

The group node carries **~2.3× the relative magnitude of any individual leaf**. Separately this
quantifies a piece of dead weight: the **root is exactly inert** — it exists before the first
insertion, so it never enters `_open`, never trains, and contributes precisely zero while still
costing 551K allocated parameters. (In the unified framing of [UNIFIED.md](UNIFIED.md) that is not a
bug so much as an empty slot: the backbone is already playing the root's role from outside the tree.)

*Jointly formed.* N36 attributes each node to the pool instances that shaped it, by
`‖∂L(x_i)/∂(A_n,B_n)‖` under the consolidation loss itself. At 8 concepts, the top-influence
instances of a leaf come from exactly one concept, and those of an internal node come from **four
different concepts** — normalized member entropy **0.61**, against ~0.0 if one member dominated. The
picture is in `figures/extra/f13_hierarchy_probe.png`, colour-coded by source concept.

So the structure is not an illusion: shared nodes exist, carry signal, and are built from several
members' data.

### 24b. But routing a newcomer through the family does not help it

N34 gives a family of held-out slots sharing a colour pair, batch-adds K of them (creating and
sealing a group node), then presents the (K+1)-th and consolidates **one identical pool** four ways.
The fourth arm is the control that makes it a test: `wrong-family` forces the newcomer through the
ancestor of a *different* family, so "any ancestor helps" and "*this* ancestor helps" can be told
apart. Mean F1 over the budget grid:

| arm | mean F1 |
|---|---|
| `no-reuse` (flat, no ancestor) | **0.091** |
| `wrong-family` (mismatched ancestor) | 0.028 |
| `reuse` (matched family ancestor) | 0.024 |

**Shared semantics are worth −0.004 F1 over a mismatched ancestor, and having any ancestor at all is
worth −0.063 over having none.** The matched and mismatched ancestors are indistinguishable, and both
are worse than no ancestor. On this evidence the hierarchy is not transferring structure to a
newcomer; if anything the extra always-on delta is interference.

One seed, one family definition, and F1 values small enough that the ordering deserves replication
before it is leaned on — but it is the direction, and the control is what makes it interpretable at
all. Without `wrong-family`, "reuse beats standalone" would have been reportable as transfer.

### 24c. Depth is not organizing abstraction either

The falsifiable prediction attached to the hierarchy was that shared ancestors act at **coarse**
resolutions and private leaves at fine ones. Measured as the ‖BA‖/‖W‖-weighted centroid over wrapped
layers in module order: **internal 48.8 vs leaf 46.9** — ancestors act, if anything, marginally
*later*. Whatever the shared node is encoding, it is not a coarse-to-fine decomposition
(`figures/extra/f13b_layer_profiles.png`).

### 24d. Capacity is not the lever either (rank sweep, 3 seeds)

`independent` beats us on the retention table at fewer parameters, and rank had been set to 8 once
and never revisited — so the comparison was ours-at-an-arbitrary-capacity against a baseline at its
own. `independent` is this same bank with flat placement, so it has the **identical** rank knob and
had to be swept too.

| rank | ours ACC | independent ACC | Δ | ours params | indep params |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.840 ± 0.009 | 0.849 ± 0.010 | −0.009 | 1.10M | 0.90M |
| 2 | 0.849 ± 0.015 | 0.852 ± 0.013 | −0.003 | 2.20M | 1.79M |
| 4 | 0.849 ± 0.026 | 0.845 ± 0.014 | **+0.003** | 4.41M | 3.58M |
| 8 | 0.842 ± 0.026 | 0.863 ± 0.015 | −0.021 | 8.82M | 7.16M |
| 16 | 0.819 ± 0.027 | 0.849 ± 0.015 | −0.030 | 17.64M | 14.33M |

**Bit-identity is 1.000 at every rank for both arms**, which is the prediction: preservation is
structural and has nothing to do with how large the delta is.

The loss to `independent` **stands** — at matched rank we lead only at r=4, by less than a standard
error. And a single-seed read taken earlier, that ACC decreases monotonically with rank
(0.844 → 0.766), **did not replicate**: at three seeds the curve is flat from r=1 to r=8 and only
falls at r=16. It is recorded here because it was nearly written up.

### 24f. Why: the tree is a caterpillar, not a hierarchy (N38)

The three negatives above have a single structural cause, and drawing the tree makes it obvious
(`figures/extra/f14_hierarchy.png`). After 40 OOD concepts on the standard backbone:

| | |
|---|---|
| nodes | 41 |
| depth | 4 |
| mean branching | **10.00** |
| shared internal nodes | **3** |
| concepts sharing a non-root ancestor | 98% |

That last number sounds like the hierarchy working and is in fact the opposite. The actual shape:

```
node 0  (40 beneath, 1 child)
  └ node 1  (40 beneath, 4 children)
      └ node 3  (36 beneath, 4 children)
          └ node 7  (32 beneath, 31 children)   <-- everything lands here
```

**31 of 40 concepts have the identical path prefix `(0, 1, 3, 7)`, and there are only 4 distinct
prefixes in total.** The tree is a *caterpillar*: a short spine with one enormous fan-out at the
bottom. There are no families. A "shared ancestor" is shared by essentially every concept, so it is a
second root rather than a family node — and the structural learning curves say this is the fixed
point, not a transient: shared nodes saturate at **3** by the tenth concept and never grow again,
depth saturates at 4, while branching climbs linearly to 10 as one node absorbs every arrival.

The mechanism is in `_descend`, and it is the interaction of two caps rather than a coding error:

```python
if ll(best, mu) < ll(cur, mu) + margin and len(children[cur]) < branch_max:
    break                      # stay here
path.append(best)              # otherwise DESCEND
```

Descent happens when the best child fits better **or** when the current node has `branch_max`
children. So a node saturates at 4 children and then forces every subsequent arrival downward
*regardless of fit* — the fan-out cap, meant to create levels, instead creates a chain. `max_depth=3`
then stops the descent, and the deepest node has no cap that can fire, so it accumulates without
limit. Chain plus dump.

This retro-explains the rest of §24 without needing separate stories. **§24b** finds no transfer
because the "family ancestor" a newcomer routes through is shared by 32 of 40 concepts and therefore
encodes nothing family-specific — routing through it and routing through a *different* spine node are
close to the same operation, which is exactly what the `wrong-family` control measured. **§24c**
finds no coarse-to-fine organization because there is only one spine, not a hierarchy of abstraction
levels. And **§5s** found clustered placement worst because the placement rule was never clustering.

It also sharpens §23. All **5** arrival orders produce *identical* shape statistics — 41 nodes, depth
4, branching 10.00, 3 shared, mean path 4.62 — while §23 measured only 5.6% of concepts keeping their
path. Those are consistent and worth stating together: **order changes which concept lands where; it
does not change the shape.** The caterpillar is what this rule builds from this data, always.

**What would have to change.** Not the sealing discipline, which is untouched by any of this — the
placement **criterion**. This paragraph originally named `branch_max` as the specific defect; §24g–j
measured it and that emphasis was wrong. Saturation-forced descent is real (36% of descents) but it
is a *symptom*: swapping the nearest-child criterion for category utility drives it to 0% and makes
the caps irrelevant. Cobweb's merge/split would still be the principled completion and is still
forbidden — but it turns out to be worth ~0.01 purity here against the criterion's 0.33 (§24j). The
failures measured above are **failures of this placement rule**, not evidence that hierarchical
sharing cannot help.

### 24g. The criterion, not the caps: what a placement rule would have to be (N39)

§24f blamed the caps from reading the code. Planting the answer settles it
(`tests/diag_placement_synthetic.py`): **four well-separated Gaussian families of ten, 64 dimensions,
centres ~6 apart** — ancestor purity 1.00 is achievable by construction, chance is 0.25.

| rule | branch_max | max_depth | biggest shared prefix | **ancestor purity** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `ll` (*former default*) | 4 | 3 | **31 / 40** | **0.28** |
| `ll` | 16 | 6 | 16 / 40 | 0.49 |
| `cu` | 4 | 3 | 32 / 40 | **0.62** |
| `cu` | 16 | 6 | 32 / 40 | 0.62 |

**The shipped rule scores 0.28 against a chance of 0.25 on data with perfect planted structure**, and
reproduces the caterpillar exactly — 31 of 40 on one prefix, the same figure N38 measured on real
teacher means. That disposes of the alternative explanation completely: the degeneracy is intrinsic
to the rule and has nothing to do with whether ColorMNIST's means cluster.

It also corrects §24f's emphasis. Raising the caps helps the `ll` rule (0.28 → 0.49) but **category
utility more than doubles purity at the shipped caps** (0.28 → 0.62) and makes the caps irrelevant
(0.62 either way). The primary defect is the criterion, not the fan-out limit:

* `ll` asks *"which existing child is nearest?"* — a point-to-point distance that can only ever route
  a concept toward something, never decide it belongs on its own;
* `cu` asks *"which partition of this node's children is most informative?"* — a partition-level
  score, so "start a new group" is a move it can choose. That is what produces balance, and it is
  what Cobweb has that this tree threw away.

Two further defects in `_ll` are visible by inspection and are folded into the `cu` arm: a single
global scalar `var`, so a node holding forty diverse concepts scores as tight as a singleton (Cobweb
uses each node's own variance from its sufficient statistics), and a `mean` over dimensions where
Cobweb sums, shrinking every likelihood difference by 1/D.

### 24h. What insert-only costs on its own — and a re-reading of §5s

The same probe isolates the part no criterion can fix. Presenting the identical families **grouped**
(all of family 0, then family 1, …) versus **shuffled**:

| rule | grouped | shuffled | gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ll` | 0.49 | 0.47 | +0.02 |
| `cu` | 0.62 | **0.81** | **−0.19** |

**Shuffled is better**, and substantially so once the criterion can act on it. The reason is
structural: grouped arrival puts ten members of one family into the tree before any other family
exists, so the top-level split forms around a single family and every later family has to hang off
it. Cobweb repairs exactly this with merge and split; an insert-only tree cannot, and §24f explains
why we cannot add them — they restructure nodes that already own sealed adapter parameters.

This re-reads a result that has sat in the repository as evidence against the hierarchy. **§5s found
`shuffled` (0.859) beats `clustered` (0.831)** and called it "the exact opposite of the hierarchy's
premise, in the exact case built to favour it." The synthetic probe reproduces the same ordering on
planted data with the same sign, which means §5s was not measuring *hierarchy is useless* — it was
measuring **insert-only is hurt by grouped arrival**. Those are different claims, and the second one
is a property of the constraint we adopted for preservation rather than of hierarchical sharing.

### 24i. Where this leaves the redesign

Best achieved here is **0.81** (cu, shuffled) against an achievable **1.00**, so a criterion swap
does not close it either. Honest accounting of the three causes, in order of size:

| cause | evidence | fixable while insert-only? |
|---|---|---|
| nearest-child criterion instead of a partition score | 0.28 → 0.62 at fixed caps | **yes** — `rule="cu"` |
| saturation-forced descent (`branch_max`) | 0.28 → 0.49 under `ll`; irrelevant under `cu` | **yes** — raise the caps |
| no merge/split to repair early splits | grouped 0.62 vs shuffled 0.81 | **no** — forbidden by §24f |

**`cu` is now the default** (`ConceptTree`, `LIFELONG_TREE_RULE`), and `"ll"` remains selectable so
the control arm of any comparison can still name it explicitly. It is adopted for tree SHAPE, which
is what this section is about, and *not* for end-task gain — the rule comparison
(`rulecmp.sbatch`, 2 experiments x 2 rules x 3 seeds, env-set so the experiment code is
byte-identical between arms) found that effect small and **mixed**:

| | `ll` | `cu` |
|---|---|---|
| n9 showdown, ours F1 | 0.606 | **0.633** |
| n9 showdown, ours joint | 0.539 | **0.556** |
| n5 class-incremental, ACC | **0.845** | 0.826 |
| n5 parameters | **8.82 M** | 9.37 M |

Every non-bank baseline is byte-identical under both rules, which is the control that the env
override touched only the arm it was supposed to. Tables collected under `ll` and not yet re-run
are still `ll`-derived and are marked here rather than silently reattributed.

What `cu` is *not* is validated end to end: a better-shaped tree is only worth having if it makes
§24b's transfer appear, and that has to be measured rather than assumed — the same mistake the
reuse claim made before the `wrong-family` control was added.

### 24j. The fix, measured: insert-only category utility recovers 91% of an offline oracle

The §24i table said a criterion swap was available and unvalidated. It is now validated on the real
teacher means, at the scale where the caterpillar appeared (40 concepts, `n39_placement.py`):

| arm | balance | **purity (fg / bg)** | internal nodes | forced descents |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `current` (**shipped**) | 0.512 | **0.27** | 4 | **36%** |
| `caps` (bmax 16, dmax 6) | 0.851 | 0.31 | 5 | 27% |
| `nodevar` (per-node variance) | 0.637 | 0.38 | 7 | 3% |
| **`cu` insert-only** | **0.995** | **0.60** | **20** | **0%** |
| `cu` at the *shipped* caps (4 / 3) | 0.995 | 0.60 | 20 | 0% |
| `GaussianCobweb` (**with** merge + split) | 0.946 | 0.61 | 13 | — |
| `agglom` (offline oracle, sees all 40) | 0.866 | **0.66** | 39 | — |
| *random baseline* | — | *0.25* | — | — |

Three things follow.

**The shipped rule is at chance.** 0.27 against a random baseline of 0.25, on data where the offline
oracle reaches 0.66 and the control (§24g Part 1) confirms colour genuinely clusters — silhouette
+0.178 for both fg and bg in μ-space. It is not finding structure that is demonstrably there.

**Insert-only CU recovers essentially all of it.** 0.60 against full Cobweb's 0.61 — **98% of what
merge and split buy** — and 91% of the offline oracle, while remaining insert-only and therefore
compatible with the preservation certificate. The forbidden operators turn out to be worth ~0.01
purity here; the criterion was worth 0.33.

**And it needs no hyperparameter change.** `cu` at the shipped caps (branch_max 4, max_depth 3) scores
identically to `cu` at 16/6. Once the criterion can choose "start a new group", saturation-forced
descent never fires (0% forced, against 36% under `ll`) and the caps stop mattering. §24f blamed the
caps; the caps were a symptom.

The shape changes accordingly: balance 0.512 → **0.995**, and internal nodes 4 → **20**, so there are
five times as many places where sharing could occur. Redrawing N38's tree under `rule="cu"` gives
**16–17 shared nodes across every arrival order** against the 3 the shipped rule produced.

![the tree under category utility](../figures/extra/f14_hierarchy_cu.png)

The same 40 concepts, same backbone, same arrival orders — only the placement criterion differs.
Compare `figures/extra/f14_hierarchy.png`, the caterpillar the shipped rule builds from the identical
input.

**What is still not claimed.** A better-shaped tree is a means, not a result. §24b measured that
routing a newcomer through its family transfers nothing *under the caterpillar*, where the "family"
ancestor was shared by 32 of 40 concepts and so encoded nothing family-specific. Whether transfer
appears once families are real is the experiment this makes possible and it has not been run. Asserting
it would repeat exactly the error the `wrong-family` control was added to catch.

`ConceptTree(rule="cu")` is implemented with `"ll"` as the default, so no existing number in this
document moves; the full suite passes unchanged (11/11) after the change.

### 24e. What this means for the claims

The hierarchy's *structural* properties hold and are the ones the certificates rest on: sealed nodes
leave the optimizer, preservation is exact at every rank and every backbone competence (§25 C3),
deletion is exact (§22). Its *functional* claims do not survive contact:

| claim | status |
|---|---|
| shared nodes exist and carry signal | **holds** (‖BA‖ 2.3× a leaf) |
| shared nodes are formed from several members | **holds** (entropy 0.61) |
| a newcomer benefits from routing through its family | **not supported** (§24b) |
| depth organizes abstraction coarse→fine | **refuted** (§24c) |
| clustered placement beats alternatives | **refuted** earlier (§5s) |
| the tree forms families at all | **refuted** (§24f): 31/40 concepts share one path prefix |

The honest summary is that **what is load-bearing is the sealing discipline — path-scoped
optimization and private leaves — and not the tree's clustering judgment.** That is already how
[../README.md](../README.md) frames it ("the bank's value is the constraint structure, not clustering
magic"), and §24 is the evidence for that sentence rather than a new position.

## 25. Addressing, not adapting: a lifelong setting that costs 72 bytes per concept (N41)

Everything above is *additive*: a new part costs a low-rank block over a frozen backbone. That
preserves, but storage grows at 551K per concept and §24 showed the hierarchy makes it worse rather
than better. This section tests a different setting, and the argument for it is our own group's ACS
paper (Wang, Singaravadivelan & MacLellan, *Diffusion Models and Concept Formation*):

> A diffusion model's noisy marginals are Gaussian smoothings of the data distribution,
> `p_t = p_data * N(0, (1−ᾱ_t)I)`, so each noise level is a kernel density estimate at a different
> bandwidth — and the **modes** of those marginals across a ladder of noise levels form a hierarchy
> of prototypes corresponding to a Cobweb tree.

If that is right, **the concept hierarchy is already inside the frozen backbone.** We should not be
building one out of adapters; we should be *addressing* the one that is there. A concept then costs a
**pointer** — where in the model's own conditioning space it lives — not a private function.

Three storage classes, which the existing router already distinguishes (§6c):

| class | what it is | cost |
|---|---|---|
| `coord` | a concept expressible in the factor basis: one logit per factor value | **72 bytes** |
| `basis` | a genuinely new *value* of an existing factor | 1024 bytes, and §5q measured one direction covering *every* combination using that value |
| `block` | a genuinely new *factor*, or no factored description | a low-rank block — §5q measured conditioning-only routes at **0.000** here, so this class is real |

24 concepts on ColorMNIST (`n41_addressing.py`):

| arm | total storage | per concept | vs blocks | backbone | joint | **F1** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `block-every` (rank 8) | 55.115 MB | 2,296,467 B | 1× | **exact** | 0.484 | 0.572 |
| `block-r1` (rank 1) | 6.889 MB | 287,058 B | 8× | **exact** | 0.531 | **0.719** |
| **`addressing`** | **0.002 MB** | **72 B** | **31,895×** | **exact** | 0.484 | **0.699** |

**Addressing costs four orders of magnitude less and is not worse.** It matches `block-every` on joint
accuracy exactly (0.484) and *beats* it on F1 (0.699 vs 0.572), while tying `block-r1` within noise.
Twenty-four concepts cost **1.7 KB**, against 55 MB.

**Replicated over three seeds** (`slurm/jobs/n41_seeds.sbatch`, `--n-score 8`):

| arm | F1 (3 seeds) | per-seed | paired Δ vs addressing |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`addressing`** | **0.678 ± 0.042** | 0.707 / 0.618 / 0.709 | — |
| `block-r1` | 0.643 ± 0.042 | 0.701 / 0.600 / 0.629 | **+0.035**, 3/3 seeds |
| `block-every` | 0.587 ± 0.020 | 0.606 / 0.560 / 0.595 | **+0.091**, 3/3 seeds |

Addressing wins in **every seed against both blocks**, which the single-seed table above did not
show — there `block-r1` scored 0.719 and led. That reversal is exactly the seed noise §5p measured
(up to 0.07 F1 between two runs of the same command), and it is why the single-seed ordering should
not be quoted. The defensible claim is the paired one: **addressing is not worse than a block at
31,895× less storage**, with a consistent but sub-seed-spread edge over rank 1 (min paired gap
0.006) and a clean one over rank 8 (min 0.058).

### 25a. The same question with blocks kept (N40)

`addressing` removes weights entirely.  The complementary curve asks how far the *additive* setting
can be pushed before giving them up — same 24-concept stream, blocks throughout, arrangement rate
0.50 (conservative against §6c's measured rate):

| arm | params | KB/arrival | vs pile | bit-identical | joint | F1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `per-instance` (the pile) | 13.78M | 2242.6 | 1.0× | 1.000 | 0.484 | 0.560 |
| `shipped` (hierarchical, r8) | 13.78M | 2242.6 | 1.0× | 1.000 | 0.453 | 0.627 |
| `flat-r8` | 13.78M | 2242.6 | 1.0× | 1.000 | 0.484 | 0.560 |
| `flat-r1` | 1.72M | 280.3 | **8.0×** | 1.000 | 0.531 | 0.707 |
| **`flat-r1-routed`** | **1.03M** | **168.6** | **13.3×** | 1.000 | 0.500 | **0.719** |

**13.3× cheaper and the best F1 in the table** (0.719 against the pile's 0.560), with bit-identity
1.000 throughout. Rank and routing are separable and multiply: rank 1 alone is 8×, routing adds
another 1.7× at this arrangement rate, and neither costs accuracy. The hierarchical arm is the *only*
one that spends more than flat for no gain.

`backbone drift` is **0.000e+00** for every arm, but it means something stronger for `addressing`:
the other arms preserve the backbone while adding adapters to it, whereas addressing **never writes
