any parameter at all** — the entire lifelong state is a list of factor coordinates, and generation
recomputes `c = μ + Σ_f gain_f (w_f · V_f)` from the frozen basis at read time.

**What this does not claim.** It is not unbounded: §5q's limit stands, and a genuinely new *factor*
(or a concept with no factored description) still needs a block, scoring **0.000** by any
conditioning-only route. So the honest statement is that the *combinatorial* case — which routing
says is the overwhelming majority of a lifelong stream over a factored domain — is essentially free,
and the vocabulary case is not. It is also untested where factors overlap spatially (§5o).

## 26. Why K = 3?  Let the query decide (N42)

Every composition here selects a fixed **K = 3** experts, a number nobody justified and a reviewer
will ask about. It is also cognitively odd: Cobweb does not fix in advance how many concepts an
instance needs; it descends until the instance is accounted for.

Mode ascent already yields a dictionary of prototypes — in the ACS framing those modes *are* the
hierarchy's concepts. So take them greedily instead of top-3: repeatedly keep the mode that explains
most of what is still unexplained, and stop when the query is accounted for.

```
S ← {} ;  repeat:  S ← S + argmax_m expl(S ∪ {m}) ;  until expl(S) ≥ τ or the gain < ε
expl(S) = 1 − Var(x_q − μ_T(S)) / Var(x_q)
```

`μ_T(S)` is the pipeline's own per-dimension product-of-experts mean, so the reconstruction is the
object the pipeline actually goes on to use — this is matching pursuit over the model's own modes.

| candidate pool | τ | mean K | K range | expl | expl at K=3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 starts | 0.90 | 2.25 | 2–3 | 0.928 | 0.955 |
| 8 starts | **0.95** | **3.12** | 2–4 | 0.959 | 0.955 |
| 8 starts | 0.99 | 9.38 | 8–11 | 0.985 | 0.955 |
| 16 starts | 0.90 | 2.25 | 2–3 | 0.931 | 0.954 |
| 16 starts | **0.95** | **3.12** | 2–4 | 0.961 | 0.954 |
| 16 starts | 0.99 | 10.25 | 9–12 | 0.987 | 0.954 |

**K = 3 is what a 95%-explanation criterion picks.** Mean K is 3.12 at τ = 0.95, and it is
*identical at both pool sizes* — so the number is set by the stopping threshold, not by how many
candidates were generated, which is the robustness objection this invites. Composition quality is
unchanged (greedy joint 0.742 vs fixed 0.750 on the same candidate pool), so choosing K per instance
costs nothing and removes an unjustified constant.

Two honest findings against the idea:

**τ = 0.99 is unreachable.** Explained variance saturates at 0.985–0.987 and never hits 0.99, so K
runs to the cap. The mode dictionary cannot fully account for a query; asking it to is a
mis-specification, not a harder setting.

**The residual is a poor novelty detector.** The hope was that "how many concepts did this need"
would separate familiar from novel. Measured as AUROC against in-distribution queries:

| | K_chosen | residual (1 − expl) |
|---|---|---|
| τ = 0.90 | 0.562 | 0.531 |
| τ = 0.95 | 0.555 / 0.500 | 0.516 / 0.578 |
| τ = 0.99 | 0.711 / 0.648 | 0.750 / **0.844** |

Best is 0.844, against the deployed router's **0.980 / 0.995** from denoising error alone (§6c). So
greedy selection justifies K and does **not** improve routing; the density-based router stays.

## 27. The competence threshold is a product law, not a threshold (N35 re-read)

N35 found composition on the ladder is **step-like**: identically 0.000 until ~80 epochs, then it
appears. C1 was recorded as "a competence threshold" without a mechanism. A literature check supplies
one, and the data to test it was already collected.

Okawa et al. (*Compositional Abilities Emerge Multiplicatively*, NeurIPS 2023, arXiv:2310.09336)
report that compositional performance rises suddenly because it depends **multiplicatively** on the
performance of its constituents. N35 records per-factor accuracy alongside joint, so the prediction
`joint ≈ digit × fg × bg` is directly checkable:

| epochs | digit | fg | bg | **product** | **joint** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2–20 | 0.000 | 0.000–0.219 | 0.500 | 0.000 | 0.000 |
| 40 | 0.031 | 0.500 | 0.438 | 0.007 | 0.016 |
| 80 | 0.156 | 0.891 | 0.344 | 0.048 | 0.016 |
| 130 | 0.547 | 0.984 | 0.531 | 0.286 | 0.266 |
| 200 | 0.609 | 0.750 | 0.750 | 0.343 | 0.312 |

**corr(joint, product) = +0.995, mean |joint − product| = 0.023.** The law holds on this dataset and
setup, which is neither the one Okawa et al. used nor a replication attempt — it fell out of a ladder
built for a different question.

Two of N35's own findings are re-explained by it.

**C1 was never a threshold.** Nothing switches on at 80 epochs. Composition is a product of three
constituent abilities, so it is pinned near zero while *any* factor is weak and rises steeply once
all three are simultaneously adequate. The step is the product of three sigmoids, not a phase change,
and describing it as a threshold implied a mechanism that is not there.

**C4's refutation now has a reason.** N35 measured that `seen_joint` predicts downstream composition
better than `basis_r2` (+0.986 vs +0.781), against the thesis-shaped expectation. That is what a
product law predicts: `seen_joint` **is** the joint accuracy of the constituents on trained slots —
approximately the same product composition multiplies — so it is not a rival explanatory variable at
all, it is the quantity itself measured on seen data. The comparison was closer to circular than to
a test, and `basis_r2` losing it says less than N35 implied.

**What this changes operationally.** It sharpens N37's sealing criterion: the question is not "is the
backbone good enough" but "is the *weakest factor* good enough", because the product is governed by
the minimum. On this ladder `bg` sits at 0.344 while `fg` is 0.891 at the same rung, so a seal
decision reading average competence would be reading the wrong statistic.

## 28. Conditioning scheme decides composition — and decides whether our composer is needed (N43)

§13 concluded that the *granularity of the conditionals* determines whether score addition works,
from a comparison of entangled ColorMNIST against factored CelebA. That confounded the conditioning
scheme with the dataset. N43 removes the confound: **two backbones, identical data, identical UNet,
identical recipe and epoch count, differing only in how the model is told what to draw.**

`class` gives one free embedding row per compositional slot (121 rows, 30,976 params).
`factored` sums one learned embedding per (factor, value) — 30 rows, **7,680 params, 4.03× smaller** —
so the conditioning is compositional *by construction*. Both reach comparable competence: the
factored backbone scores unconditional validity 0.984 and conditional joint accuracy 0.828.

### 28a. Factored conditioning produces local conditional scores

The literature's proposed mechanism (arXiv:2509.16447) is that compositional structure is equivalent
to **local conditional scores** — sparse dependence on pixels *and* conditioners. Perturbing one
factor's conditioning contribution and measuring where `ε` moves:

| perturbed factor | top-10% pixel share | | fg/bg selectivity | |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| | **class** | **factored** | **class** | **factored** |
| digit | 0.416 | **0.487** | 4.565 | **6.526** |
| fg | 0.376 | **0.415** | 4.272 | **5.083** |
| bg | 0.251 | **0.258** | 1.263 | **2.083** |

**Factored conditioning is more spatially concentrated and more selective on every factor.** The
metric passes its own sanity check in both arms: perturbing `digit` or `fg` moves foreground pixels
4–6.5× harder than background, while perturbing `bg` does not (1.26 / 2.08) — which is the correct
answer for a background factor, and evidence the number is measuring what it claims.

### 28b. Score addition flips from useless to excellent

| scheme | arm | ideal-words R² | joint | recall | **F1** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `class` | `score-add` | 0.826 | 0.125 | 1.000 | **0.200** |
| **`factored`** | **`score-add`** | **1.000** | **0.812** | 0.868 | **0.902** |
| `class` | ours | 0.826 | 0.000 | 0.816 | 0.179 |
| `factored` | ours | 1.000 | 0.125 | 0.974 | 0.198 |

**Same dataset. Same architecture. Only the conditioning scheme changed, and score addition goes
from 0.200 to 0.902.** §13's claim survives the removal of its confound, and the mechanism in §28a
is the reason: adding scores is only meaningful when each conditional depends on its own factor
sparsely, which is what factored conditioning produces and entangled conditioning does not.

The ideal-words R² is **exactly 1.000** on the factored backbone against 0.826 on the class one —
the embedding space *is* additively factorizable, because it was built that way.

### 28c. Our composer estimates the right object — provably, for the first time

On the factored backbone the **true** per-(factor, value) embeddings exist and are readable off
`model.attr`. Our composer estimates exactly that object by ridge regression on class embeddings, so
its estimate can be scored against the truth rather than against downstream accuracy:

**cosine(estimated, true) = +1.000 for digit, +1.000 for fg, +1.000 for bg.**

The ideal-words factorization is not an approximation that happens to be useful — when a ground-truth
factor decomposition exists, ridge regression on the embeddings recovers it exactly. That validation
is impossible on an entangled backbone, and it is the strongest evidence this repository has that the
composer's *representation* is correct rather than merely effective.

### 28d. The uncomfortable consequence: our composition machinery is a workaround

Our method does **not** benefit from the better backbone — F1 0.179 → 0.198, essentially unchanged —
while score addition goes to 0.902. **On a factored backbone, plain training-free score addition
beats our composer by 4.5×.**

Read plainly: the composition half of this work is a workaround for backbones whose conditionals are
entangled. Given a model conditioned compositionally, the right thing to do is add scores and skip
the mode ascent, the PoE teacher, the factor-basis inversion and the distillation entirely. That is
consistent with §11c, where `composable-mcmc` already beat us on CelebA — the one benchmark here with
a factored head — and it now has a mechanism rather than being a benchmark-dependent curiosity.

**What this does not touch.** The lifelong half is orthogonal to all of it. Routing, addressing
(§25), preservation, deletion and batch absorption operate on whatever conditioning the backbone has;
§25's 72-bytes-per-concept result is *better* on a factored backbone, since the factor coordinates
are then exact rather than a ridge estimate. What N43 narrows is the *composition* claim, not the
lifelong one.

**Caveat on the `ours` rows.** N43 runs the `ours` arm at reduced budget (25 epochs, 6 cases) against
§11d's published configuration, so 0.179/0.198 should not be read against the headline F1 0.605. The
matched comparisons — `score-add` across schemes, and `ours` vs `score-add` *within* a scheme — are
the valid ones, and a budget difference is unlikely to close a 4.5× gap.

## 29. Why CelebA looks worse here than in the companion paper: a full setup audit

CelebA results here are visibly worse than the paper's — for **our** method and for **theirs** — and
that has been noted several times without being localized. Reading the companion paper's Appendices
B, C, D and F against our configuration finds **not one bug but fourteen differences**, three of
which are large enough to explain the image quality on their own.

### 29a. The three that dominate

| | companion paper | this repository | why it matters |
|---|---|---|---|
| **backbone initialization** | **fine-tunes `google/ddpm-celebahq-256`** (sample_size overridden to 128) | **trained from scratch** | They begin from a model that already renders photorealistic faces. We ask a 21M-parameter UNet to learn faces *and* conditioning from nothing. This is the picture-quality gap, and no downstream setting can close it. |
| **training budget** | **500,000 steps on 4× RTX 6000**, bf16, ≈50 h wall ⇒ ~200 GPU-hours | ~90 epochs on **1** A40, fp32, ≈26 GPU-hours | ~8× less compute, on a harder task. |
| **benchmark size** | **74 realized classes** — 51 seen, 23 held out; 26,775 train images ⇒ **~525 img/class** | **911–1205 combos**, 111 held out; 88,184 train images ⇒ **~97 img/class** | Our benchmark is ~12× wider with ~5× fewer images per class, so the conditioning is estimated from far less evidence and the discrimination problem is far harder. |

**These affect every arm equally, which is the point.** A weak backbone caps `poe-guided`, `ours`,
and every baseline alike — the paper's method is not being reproduced badly so much as being run on a
substrate a fraction as good.

### 29b. The rest of the audit

| axis | paper | ours | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| resolution | 128×128 | 64px (128 in training now) | being fixed |
| diffusion schedule | **linear β**, T=1000 | **cosine ᾱ** | a real schedule mismatch, repo-wide |
| precision | bf16 | fp32 | minor |
| conditioning | class-embedding table, \|C\|+1 rows (52) | **factored** (per-attribute sum) | our deliberate deviation (§n19), justified there but *not* the paper's setup |
| holdout design | each held-out class differs from a **seen** class by **one attribute** | held-out *pair* (`Smiling`×`Big_Nose`), or 24 random combos | ours is a far harder split, by design and not by accident |
| ascent timestep grid | **[50, 500, 50]** → 10 levels | `(0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.4)` → **4 levels** | we search a coarser, narrower ladder |
| ascent starts / timestep | 8 | 12 | we use more |
| ascent iterations | 100 | 80 | we use fewer |
| ascent step size | 0.09 | 0.1 | minor |
| **ascent initialization** | **query-centered** | **hybrid (50% query / 50% random)** | our documented deviation — helpful on ColorMNIST, never re-validated on CelebA |
| softmax temperature τ | **0.5 fixed** | **adaptive** | our documented deviation (`docs/SETTINGS.md` §2, and `poe_teacher`'s docstring), justified on ColorMNIST |
| queries per OOD class | **100** | far fewer | our error bars are wider than theirs |
| K prototypes | 3 | 3 | matches |
| Hutchinson probes | 4 | 4 | matches |

### 29c. What this means, and what to do

**The honest reading is that our CelebA numbers are not a reproduction of the paper's CelebA
experiment.** They share a dataset name and little else: a from-scratch 21M backbone on 12× the
classes with ~8× less compute, a different noise schedule, a different conditioning scheme, a harder
holdout, and a coarser discovery ladder. Every CelebA comparison in this document should be read as
*internally* valid — all arms share the substrate — and **not** as evidence about the companion
method's published performance.

The fix is ordered by expected effect:

1. **Use the pretrained backbone.** `google/ddpm-celebahq-256` is already cached here and §12 already
   proved our bank attaches to it with 0.000e+00 preservation. This is the single highest-value
   change and it needs no training at all.
2. **Rebuild the benchmark to the paper's shape** — ~74 realized classes, 23 holdouts chosen one
   attribute away from a seen class.
3. **Match discovery**: 10-level timestep grid, query-centered init, 100 iterations, τ = 0.5 — at
   minimum as a `--paper-exact` arm so our deviations are measured against it rather than assumed
   better.
4. Linear β and bf16, if the backbone is retrained at all.

Item 1 subsumes items 2 and 4 for image quality and is the only one that changes what the samples
look like. It is also the setup the paper actually used for its own CelebA figures.

### 29a. Two of those disparities were bugs, not choices — and §29's verdict is now unsafe

A line-by-line pass over the paper's Tables 5–8 (`docs/SETTINGS.md`) separated deliberate deviations
from accidental ones and found **four accidental**, two of which were producing published-looking
numbers.

**(a) CelebA was guided at 2.4× the published strength.** A constant named `PAPER_POE` holding
`w0 = 1.2` was copy-pasted into six experiment files. 1.2 is Table 8's **Color-MNIST** value; CelebA
is **0.5**. So n20 — the CelebA headline — plus n44 and `diag_celeba_chain` all over-guided CelebA,
*including the `poe-guided` arm*, under a comment reading "the published method in its published
configuration". Measured on the paper's own pretrained `ddpm-celebahq-256`:

| arm | w₀ = 1.2 (what we ran) | w₀ = 0.5 (Table 8) |
|---|---|---|
| prototypes (mode ascent) | 0.78 – 1.40 | unchanged (not sampled) |
| `poe-guided` | **2.50 – 4.82** | **1.45 – 1.67** |
| `query-only` | 3.53 – 4.27 | 1.80 – 2.56 |

*(tv ratio against real faces; 1.0 = as rough as real, >1 = rougher.)* The published method was being
handicapped by a factor it never chose, and correcting it roughly halves the excess roughness.

**(b) §29's verdict does not survive this run.** §29 concluded the from-scratch backbone determined
CelebA image quality. But the table above is on **their** pretrained backbone — a model that renders
photorealistic faces, untouched by us — and its `poe-guided` output is still 1.45–1.67× rougher than
real, while the mode-ascent prototypes feeding it sit at 0.78–1.40. The damage is therefore
downstream of the backbone *and* downstream of discovery.

**(c) Our own 64px backbone fails in the opposite direction**, which is the detail that redirects the
investigation. `diag_celeba_w0.py` predicted the over-guidance signature — tv > 1 — and **falsified
it**: both arms come out at tv **0.727 (w₀=0.5)** and **0.781 (w₀=1.2)**, i.e. *smoother* than real,
not rougher. Our CelebA samples are blurry, not over-guided. Guidance strength is not what is wrong
with them.

So the CelebA picture-quality question has three candidate causes and §29 named only the third:

1. the w₀ infidelity — **real, measured, fixed** (it damages the pretrained path most)
2. the input-layout permutation — **§31**, and the leading candidate for the blur
3. the from-scratch backbone — still plausible, no longer established

**(d) Cause 3 is now settled, and it is worse than "picture quality" (2026-08-12).** Two CelebA
backbones were trained from scratch at the paper's own 128 px, 90 epochs each (`ddpm_celeba128.pt`
and the no-pair split), specifically to remove resolution as a confound. Both completed, and in both
**the class conditioning never took hold**:

| | real | conditional | unconditional | margin |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| per-attribute accuracy | 0.761 | **0.442** | **0.444** | **−0.003** |
| per-attribute (no-pair split) | 0.720 | **0.418** | **0.436** | **−0.018** |
| joint accuracy | 0.223 | **0.000** | — | **0 % of ceiling** |

Conditioning on a class produces output statistically indistinguishable from not conditioning at all,
on both splits. At this compute budget a from-scratch CelebA backbone does not learn the conditional
the entire composition benchmark is defined over — so every CelebA arm on such a backbone was being
compared on a substrate that cannot express the task, and no downstream setting could have fixed it.

**This retroactively justifies the N47 route as necessary rather than convenient.** Fine-tuning
`google/ddpm-celebahq-256` (their Table 5 configuration) *does* learn it: N48's conditioning check
reads RMS 0.0379 between two class conditionals against 0.0821 to the null token, and its arms reach
joint 0.500 where the from-scratch model reaches 0.000. The paper fine-tunes a pretrained model, and
this is the measurement showing that choice is load-bearing, not incidental.

**The CelebA tables have now been rerun at the corrected w₀**, both splits × 3 seeds in the canonical
configuration (`--comp-scale 7.5 --mcmc-steps 10 --mcmc-lam 0.3`). The result is the cleanest possible
confirmation that the fix was correctly scoped: **only the `poe-guided` rows moved.** Every other arm
— ours, `composable-diff`, `composable-mcmc`, `query-only`, `nearest-class`, `real` — is unchanged to
three decimals, because `w₀` is the analytic-PoE guidance strength and no other arm uses it.

| CelebA, pair split | w₀ = 1.2 (what we ran) | w₀ = 0.5 (Table 8) |
|---|---|---|
| `poe-guided` pair | 0.645 ± 0.189 | 0.574 ± 0.212 |
| `poe-guided` recall | 0.000 ± 0.000 | **0.004 ± 0.002** |
| `poe-guided` F1 | 0.000 ± 0.000 | **0.008 ± 0.004** |
| `poe-guided` diversity | 0.147 ± 0.004 | **0.405 ± 0.001** |

Run at its own published guidance the companion method is **less collapsed** — diversity nearly
triples and recall leaves exactly zero for the first time — but the qualitative finding is unchanged:
it still has essentially no coverage of the held-out composition. That is the right kind of outcome
for a fairness correction. It mattered, it moved the baseline in the baseline's favour, and no
comparative claim in this repository rested on the collapse being total.

**A caution recorded because it nearly became a silent error.** The first rerun used the experiment's
*default* flags and wrote to `n20_celeba_composition.json` / `_pair.json`. Those are **alias
targets**: `reproduce.sh` runs the experiment with `--comp-scale 7.5 --mcmc-steps 10 --mcmc-lam 0.3`
under `_full`/`_pairfull` and aliases the results into those names. The rerun therefore replaced a
3-seed canonical table with a 1-seed off-configuration one and **dropped the `composable-mcmc` arm
entirely** (`--mcmc-steps` defaults to 0, which disables it). It went unnoticed because
`results/aggregate.json` is not rebuilt automatically, so `test_claims` was checking the prose
against a stale aggregate and passing 37/37 while the files on disk disagreed. Aliases restored,
outputs parked as `*_w05probe.json`. **Rebuild the aggregate before trusting `test_claims`.**

## 30. The UNet has been trained on a pixel permutation the whole time (N45)

Flat rows here are HWC; every consumer hands one to the UNet with a plain `.reshape(-1, 3, H, W)`.
That is not an image. `tests/diag_layout.py` establishes it by exact index algebra, no model
involved — for 64×64×3 the network's three "channels" are image **rows** 0–21 / 21–42 / 42–63, its
width axis is the **colour** axis, and its vertical axis is a **~22-pixel jump across the picture**,
so a single 3×3 convolution reads real columns 3, 24 and 45 at once. `figures/extra/f20_layout.png` shows
a real face as the UNet sees it: a striped, colour-scrambled field with only low frequencies intact.

![what the UNet actually convolves](../figures/extra/f20_layout.png)

`ddpm.py` had recorded this deliberately and argued it was harmless, because training and sampling
share the permutation so "the model simply learns in the permuted space". That is correct for a
permutation-equivariant model and **wrong for a CNN**, whose entire inductive bias is locality.
Images still *display* correctly, which is why it survived; what cannot survive is local texture.

Proving the prior is destroyed is not proving it matters — a UNet can fit a permuted dataset by brute
force. N45 measures it: two backbones identical in architecture, data, seed, epochs, batch, lr and
schedule, differing **only** in layout, at the real recipe (160 slots, `n_per_slot` 200, 60 epochs).

| | `permuted` (every backbone to date) | `hwc` (a real image) | |
|---|---|---|---|
| held-out ε-prediction loss | 0.01410 | **0.01012** | **−28.2 %** |
| joint composition accuracy, trained slots | **0.724** | 0.661 | −0.062 |
| paired over the same 24 slots | — | 4 better / **13 worse** / 7 tied | |
| tv ratio vs real | **1.154** | 1.166 | +0.012 |

**The training objective improves by ~28 % and the composition accuracy does not — it gets worse.**
Both halves matter and they point in opposite directions.

The loss result is solid and replicated: −28.7 % in a first run and −28.2 % here, on the same
held-out split for both arms.

**The accuracy result reverses under a correct test, and that reversal is mine to own.** The first
run drew its evaluation slots *inside* the arm loop, so the RNG advanced between arms and the two
layouts were scored on **different held-out compositions**. That unpaired comparison read +0.031.
Drawing the slots once, before the loop, and scoring both arms on the identical 24 compositions
gives **−0.062**, with `hwc` losing 13 of 24 and winning 4. The apparent gain was an artefact of
comparing different data.

**So the honest statement is narrower than "fix the layout and everything improves."** Restoring the
convolutional prior buys a large, replicated improvement in the *denoising objective* and does not
buy conditional composition accuracy on this benchmark; on the paired test it costs some. A plausible
reading — untested — is that a network denied spatial structure leans harder on the conditioning
signal, because class identity is the only structure left to it, while a network that can actually
model the image distribution relies on it less. That is a hypothesis, not a result.

**What this does to the retraining question.** It weakens it considerably. A 28 % better objective is
worth having and is the correct way to train a CNN, but the case for spending ~26 GPU-hours per
CelebA backbone rested on expecting downstream quality to follow, and on this evidence it does not
follow automatically.

The roughness metric barely moves (1.154 → 1.166), and a shorter 30-epoch run reproduced the loss
effect almost exactly (−29.5 %), so the loss half is not a single-run artefact.

**Does the LOSS effect grow on a texture-rich dataset?** Color-MNIST is the *easiest possible case*
for a scrambled CNN — a near-binary stroke on flat colour, almost all low-frequency, exactly the content a
permuted network can still fit. CelebA is the opposite: dense local texture is most of the signal.
The mechanism therefore predicts a substantially larger effect there.

**Attempted, and not resolved.** A matched 12-epoch CelebA pair
(`slurm/jobs/n45_celeba.sbatch`) shows training loss 0.0728 → 0.0446, i.e. **−38.7 %**, larger than
Color-MNIST's as predicted. But that number cannot be quoted, because a 12-epoch run is **468
optimizer steps against an EMA time constant of 1000**, so the saved checkpoint retained **62.6 % of
its random initialization** — which is why evaluating it returned a loss twelve times the training
loss and a spurious −4.6 %. The training-loss comparison is between live weights and is suggestive;
the checkpoint comparison is worthless. `train_ddpm` now ramps the EMA decay (ADM/Karras warmup) and
warns on short runs, and a clean CelebA measurement needs a run long enough for the EMA to converge.
Two earlier CelebA diagnostics are confounded the same way — the 15-epoch de-risk backbone is 585
steps and 55.7 % initialization.

**What this does and does not touch.** It does not touch any storage claim — 72 bytes against
2.3 MB is arithmetic on tensor shapes. It does not touch bit-identity or the preservation guarantee,
which are properties of optimizer scoping. What it does touch is every *image-quality* number
produced on one of our own backbones. Whether it *explains* §29a's finding that our CelebA samples
are too **smooth** (tv 0.727–0.781, blurrier than real) is a hypothesis and is now weaker than it
looked: the mechanism is right in kind — a network that cannot use spatial locality is one that
renders low frequencies and omits texture — but on Color-MNIST the fix moved the roughness statistic
by 0.012, which is nothing. It remains untested where it would matter.

The fix is available and off by default (`layout="hwc"`), recorded per checkpoint, because turning it
on silently invalidates every existing checkpoint — a model must be sampled in the layout it was
trained in.

## 31. Liu et al. (ICCV 2023) unsupervised concept discovery: an attempted baseline, and why its numbers are not quotable (N46)

**Which Liu et al.** This repository already runs `composable-diff` / `composable-mcmc`, which are
**Liu et al. 2022** (Composable Diffusion): they compose concepts they are *given*. The **2023**
follow-up *discovers* them from unlabelled pixels, which makes it the closest published baseline to
what this project claims — it lands on the same shape of answer we do:

> them — a shared library of K concept embeddings + a per-image weight vector on the simplex
> us — a shared factor basis + a per-concept address (factor coordinates)

Both store a small coordinate against a shared library and leave the backbone frozen, so the storage
axis is directly comparable: their per-query cost is K floats against our 72 bytes.

**The result, with its control.**

| arm | SEEN control | held-out | |
|---|---|---|---|
| **identity check** (library = the true class embedding, `w` one-hot) | — | **0.802** | the machinery is correct |
| K = 8 | 0.010 | 0.010 | at chance |
| K = 18 (matched to 10+4+4 factor values) | 0.021 | 0.010 | at chance |
| K = 18, simplex constraint dropped (*ablation, not the paper*) | 0.031 | 0.021 | at chance |
| *chance* | 0.006 | 0.006 | |

**These numbers must not be reported as a comparison, and the SEEN control is why.** The control
applies the same library and the same weight fitting to compositions *inside the discovery pool's own
distribution*. It sits at chance. A method that cannot reproduce what it was fitted on is not being
measured on out-of-distribution composition — the port is failing, and any held-out number it emits
is uninterpretable. Quoting "ours 0.539 against theirs 0.010" would be a fabricated win.

**The composition machinery is not the fault.** The identity check settles that without involving
discovery: put the backbone's own class embedding in as a library of one and make `w` one-hot, and
Eq. 11 reduces to the ordinary class conditional. It scores **0.802** against chance 0.006. Score
composition, the sampling hook and the layout handling are all correct.

**Two things do bear on it, one structural and one about the setting.**

*Structural.* Under their constraint Σ_k w_k = 1, the unconditional term of Eq. 11 cancels exactly:

```
eps_phi + sum_k w_k (eps_k - eps_phi)  =  sum_k w_k eps_k
```

so the composed score is a **convex combination** — a weighted *average* of conditionals, not a
product. That suits what the paper uses it for (deciding which of K concepts an image is, or
splitting a scene into complementary global and local aspects) and it cannot express a
**conjunction**: a ColorMNIST composition is digit AND foreground AND background at once, and weights
summing to one cannot hold three factors at full strength simultaneously. Dropping the constraint
does move both numbers up (control 0.021 → 0.031, held-out 0.010 → 0.021) — so it is a contributing
cause — but not off the floor, so it is not the whole story.

*The setting, and this is the honest headline.* **The method's key enabler is absent on our
backbones, and the paper says so itself.** They parameterize each concept as a word embedding in a
pretrained text-to-image model, and give the reason directly: *"Directly discovering these score
functions from scratch using a dataset of images is difficult as there may be substantial ambiguity
on how images should be factored, with this difficulty compounded by the small dataset"*, and *"the
semantic space of text eliminates a lot of ambiguity when discovering concepts."* Our backbone offers
a **120-row class-embedding table trained from scratch** — precisely the regime their
parameterization exists to avoid.

**So the correct statement is that this baseline cannot be run faithfully here, not that it loses.**
A fair comparison would require putting both methods on a backbone with a pretrained semantic
conditioning space. That is a real gap in our baseline coverage and is recorded as one rather than
resolved in our favour by a number that does not mean what it appears to mean.

`lifelong_ood/neural/unsup_concepts.py` implements Algorithm 1 and Eq. 11 directly, with the simplex
kept and `simplex=False` available as the ablation; `experiments/n46_unsup_concepts.py` runs it with
the control and the identity check.

## 8. Where the numbers come from

| experiment | file | scale run |
|---|---|---|
| E0 tuning of record | `experiments/e0_tune.py` | 40 slots teacher, 6 slots sampler |
| E1 manifold validity | `experiments/e1_manifold.py` | 40 img/slot |
| E2 composition | `experiments/e2_composition.py` | 40 slots × 32 samples |
| E3 analytic lifelong | `experiments/e3_lifelong.py` | 12 concepts, both strategies |
| N0 neural sampler diagnosis | `experiments/n0_tune.py` | 6 slots |
| N1 backbone training | `experiments/n1_train.py` | 200 img/slot, 200 epochs |
| N2 neural composition | `experiments/n2_composition.py` | 12 slots × 24 samples |
| N3 neural lifelong | `experiments/n3_lifelong.py` | 10 concepts × 3 arms |
| per-concept retention analysis | `experiments/analyze_n3.py` | — |
| multi-seed pooling | `experiments/aggregate.py` | — |

Raw outputs land in `results/*.json` (git-ignored; regenerate with the commands in the README).
